Abstract
Identification of possible transformations of quantum objects including quantum states and quantum operations is indispensable in developing quantum algorithms.
Universal transformations, defined as input-independent transformations, appear in various quantum applications.
Such is the case for universal transformations of unitary operations.
However, extending these transformations to non-unitary operations is nontrivial and largely unresolved.
Addressing this, we introduce
isometry adjointation
protocols that transform an input isometry operation into its adjoint operation, which include both unitary operation and quantum state transformations.
The paper details the construction of parallel and sequential isometry adjointation protocols, derived from unitary inversion protocols using quantum combs and the (dual) Clebsch-Gordan transforms, and achieving optimal approximation error.
This error is shown to be independent of the output dimension of the isometry operation.
In particular, we explicitly obtain an asymptotically optimal parallel protocol achieving an approximation error  \epsilon=\Theta(d^{2}/n) , where  d  is the input dimension of the isometry operation and  n  is the number of calls of the isometry operation.
The research also extends to isometry inversion and universal error detection, employing semidefinite programming to assess optimal performances.
The findings suggest that the optimal performance of general protocols in isometry adjointation and universal error detection is not dependent on the output dimension, and that indefinite causal order protocols offer advantages over sequential ones in isometry inversion and universal error detection.

Introduction
The possibility and impossibility of universal transformation of unknown quantum states have played a major role in quantum information and foundations (e.g., state cloning
[
1
]
, universal NOT
[
2
]
, and swap test
[
3
]
or programmable projective measurement
[
4
]
).
Recently, transformations of quantum operations, namely,
quantum supermaps
, have been investigated to aim for
higher-order quantum computataion
[
5
]
, which is a candidate for the quantum generalization of higher-order functions
[
6
]
, as well as its connections to channel resource theory
[
7
]
, quantum thermodynamics
[
8
]
, and causal structure
[
9
]
.
Since universal transformation of quantum operations can be utilized as an elementary operation in higher-order quantum computation, the full characterization of possible universal transformations of quantum operations is indispensable.
Although universal transformations of unitary operations have been extensively studied
[
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,

,
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
]
, their generalization to non-unitary operations have not been well investigated except for a few examples (Ref.
[
35
]
for POVM measurements and Ref.
[
36
]
for isometry operations).
In particular, isometry operations represent encoding of quantum information
[
37
]
or general quantum channel via the Stinespring dilation
[
38
]
, and universal transformation of them would be useful as an elementary building block in higher-order quantum computation.
This work extends one of the most important task of universal transformation of unitary operations, called
unitary inversion
[
10
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
25
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
]
, to isometry operations.
Unitary inversion is a task to transform an unknown unitary operation  \mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{in}}  into its inverse operation  \mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1} , where  \mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{in}}  is given by  \mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{in}}(\cdot)\coloneqq U_{\mathrm{in}}\cdot U_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}  for a unitary operator  U_{\mathrm{in}} .
Since the inverse of a unitary operator  U_{\mathrm{in}}  can be given by the adjoint operator  U_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger} , one natural extension of unitary inversion to isometry operations is given by
isometry adjointation
.
We consider an isometry operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}(\cdot)\coloneqq V_{\mathrm{in}}\cdot V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger} , where  V_{\mathrm{in}}:\mathbb{C}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}^{D}  is an isometry operator satisfying  V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}V_{\mathrm{in}}=\mathds{1}_{d}  for the identity operator  \mathds{1}_{d} .
We denote the set of isometry operators  V_{\mathrm{in}}:\mathbb{C}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}^{D}  by  \mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D) , and the set of  d -dimensional unitary operators by  \mathbb{U}(d)=\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,d) .
Isometry adjointation is a task to transform an unknown isometry operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}  to a quantum instrument  \{\Phi_{a}\}_{a\in\{I,O\}}
1
1
1
Measurement outcomes  a\in\{I,O\}  stand for “in  {\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}} ” and “out of  {\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}} .”
such that  \Phi_{I}  approximates the adjoint operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger} , where  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}  is given by  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}(\cdot)\coloneqq V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}\cdot V_{\mathrm{in}} .
The adjoint operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}(\cdot)  is given as
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}})=\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}(\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}),
(1)
where  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}  is the inverse operation satisfying  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}(\rho)=\rho  for all  \rho\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d}) , and  \Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}  is the orthogonal projector onto  {\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}} .
It implements the projective measurement  \{\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathds{1}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}\}  and the inverse operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}  at the same time (see Fig.
1
).
Isometry adjointation can be reduced to two relevant tasks called
isometry inversion
[
36
]
and
universal error detection
, where the former is the task to implement the inverse operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1} , and the latter is the task to implement the projective merasurement  \{\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathds{1}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}\} , from an unknown isometry operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}} .
Figure 1:
Definition of the task isometry adjointation.
We construct parallel and sequential protocols for isometry adjointation by converting the corresponding unitary inversion protocol (Fig.
2
and Theorems
5
and
4
).
Isometry inversion and universal error detection protocols are constructed by discarding the measurement outcome and output quantum state, respectively, from the isometry adjointation protocol (Corollaries
6
and
7
). We analyze the optimality of the constructed protocols, and we show the following properties:
•
Our construction of parallel and sequential protocols gives the optimal performances among all parallel or sequential protocols (Theorem
10
).
•
Optimal parallel protocol for universal error detection is explicitly given (Theorem
12
).
•
A parallel protocol for isometry adjointation is given, which achieves an asymptotically optimal approximation error  \epsilon=\Theta(d^{2}n^{-1})  (Theorem
14
).
We also give semidefinite programming (SDP) to obtain the optimal performances for these tasks using parallel, sequential, and general protocols including indefinite causal order
[
39
,
9
,
40
]
(Section
4.3
).
The rest of this work is organized as follows.
Section
2
defines the tasks isometry adjointation, isometry inversion, and universal error detection, and corresponding figures of merit.
Section
3.1
introduces the technical details to obtain the main result of this paper (Theorem
4
) in Section
3.2
, constructing the isometry adjointation protocol.
Section
3.3
constructs isometry inversion and universal error detection protocols from the isometry adjointation protocol.
Section
4.1
introduces the Choi operator of the general quantum supermap and shows that the Choi operators corresponding to optimal protocols for isometry adjointation, isometry inversion, and universal error detection have the unitary group symmetry.
Section
4.2
analyzes the optimal performances using analytical methods based on group theory.
Section
4.3
numerically investigates the optimal performances using semidefinite programming.
Section
5
discusses potential applications of this work in other fields of quantum information.
Section
6
concludes the work.
Figure 2:
Left panel (unitary inversion): Transformation of a unitary operation  U_{\mathrm{in}}  into its inverse operation  U_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1} . Right panel (isometry adjointation): Transformation of an isometry operation  V_{\mathrm{in}}  into its adjoint operation  V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger} . The isometry adjointation protocol is constructed by converting the unitary inversion protocol (shown in blue) using a quantum comb shown in red.
2
Problem setting
We consider the following tasks for the given unknown input state  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{D})  and the unknown isometry operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}(\cdot)\coloneqq V_{\mathrm{in}}\cdot V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}  for  V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)  (see Sections
2.1
–
2.3
for the detail):
•
Probabilistic exact isometry inversion
Promise: The input state  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}  is within the code space  \mathcal{L}({\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}) .
Task: Output the quantum state  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}})  with probability  p  for any input state  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}({\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}})  and input operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}} .
•
Deterministic isometry inversion
Promise: The input state  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}  is within the code space  \mathcal{L}({\mathrm{Im}}V) .
Task: Output a quantum state close to  \mathcal{V}^{-1}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}})  for any input state  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}({\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}})  and input operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}} .
•
Universal error detection
Task: Output the measurement outcome  a\in\{I,O\}
2
2
2
I  stands for “in  {\mathrm{Im}}V ” and  O  stands for “out of  {\mathrm{Im}}V .”
with probability close to  \Tr(\Pi_{\Im V_\mathrm{in}} \rho_\mathrm{in})  ( a=I ), and  \Tr[(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}})\rho_{\mathrm{in}}]  ( a=O ) for any input state  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{D})  and input operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}} , where  \Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}  is the orthogonal projectors onto the subspace  {\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}} .
•
Isometry adjointation
Task: Output the quantum state probabilistically with the measurement outcome  a\in\{I,O\}  with probability close to  \Tr(\Pi_{\Im V_\mathrm{in}} \rho_\mathrm{in})  ( a=I ), and  \Tr[(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}})\rho_{\mathrm{in}}]  ( a=O ) for any input state  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{D})  and input operation  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}} . When  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}({\mathrm{Im}}V) , the measurement outcome  a=I  is obtained with a unit probability with an output quantum state close to  \mathcal{V}^{-1}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}}) . When the measurement outcome  a=O  is obtained, an output quantum state is given by a fixed quantum state, e.g., the maximally mixed state.
We define the details of the task in the following subsections.
To this, we introduce the notion of quantum supermaps, representing the transformations of quantum channels. We first consider a deterministic transformation from  n  quantum channels  \Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(i)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{i})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{i})  for  i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}  to a quantum channel  \Phi_{\mathrm{out}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}) , where  \mathcal{I}_{i} ,  \mathcal{O}_{i} ,  \mathcal{P} , and  \mathcal{F}  are Hilbert spaces
3
3
3
\mathcal{P}  stands for “global past” and  \mathcal{F}  stands for “global future.”
. Such a transformation is represented as a linear supermap
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}[\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{i})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{i})]\to[\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})],
(2)
where  [\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{2})]  is a set of linear maps from  \mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})  to  \mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{2}) . A probabilistic transformation of quantum channels is represented as a set  \{\mathcal{C}_{a}\}_{a} , where  a  corresponds to the classical outcome.
We consider three classes of transformations, as shown below.
•
Parallel protocol
We call  \mathcal{C}  is implemented by a parallel protocol when input operations are used in parallel as follows:
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\right]=\Lambda^{(2)}\circ\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(i)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\right]\circ\Lambda^{(1)},
(3)
where  \Lambda^{(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{A})  and  \Lambda^{(2)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})  are quantum channels,  \mathcal{A}  is an auxiliary Hilbert space, and  \mathcal{I}^{n}  and  \mathcal{O}^{n}  are joint Hilbert spaces defined by  \mathcal{I}^{n}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{I}_{i}  and  \mathcal{O}^{n}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{i} , respectively. Similarly, we consider the implementation of a probabilistic transformation of  \{\mathcal{C}_{a}\}_{a}  in a parallel protocol given by
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{a}\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\right]=\Lambda^{(2)}_{a}\circ\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(i)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\right]\circ\Lambda^{(1)},
(4)
where  \{\Lambda^{(2)}_{a}\}_{a}  is a quantum instrument
4
4
4
A quantum instrument is a set of completely-positive maps that sum up to a completely-positive and trace-preserving map
[
41
]
.
.
We also consider a subclass of parallel protocols called parallel delayed input-state protocols, which are realized as
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\right](\rho_{\mathrm{in}})=\Lambda\left[\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(i)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\right)(\phi)\right],
(5)
where  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})  is an input quantum state and  \phi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{A})  is a quantum state. Similarly, we consider the parallel delayed input-state protocol of a probabilistic transformation given by
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{a}\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\right](\rho_{\mathrm{in}})=\Lambda_{a}\left[\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(i)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\right)(\phi)\right].
(6)
•
Sequential protocol (quantum comb
[
42
]
)
We call  \mathcal{C}  is implemented by a sequential protocol or a quantum comb
[
42
]
when input operations are used sequentially as follows:
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\right]=\Lambda^{(n+1)}\circ\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}\right]\circ\Lambda^{(n)}\circ\cdots\circ\Lambda^{(2)}\circ\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\right]\circ\Lambda^{(1)},
(7)
where  \Lambda^{(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{1}),\Lambda^{(2)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{2}),\ldots,\Lambda^{(n)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n-1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n}),\Lambda^{(n+1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})  are quantum channels and  \mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{n}  are auxiliary Hilbert spaces. Similarly, we consider the implementation of a probabilistic transformation  \{\mathcal{C}_{a}\}_{a}  in a sequential protocol given by
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{a}\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\right]=\Lambda^{(n+1)}_{a}\circ\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}\right]\circ\Lambda^{(n)}\circ\cdots\circ\Lambda^{(2)}\circ\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\right]\circ\Lambda^{(1)},
(8)
where  \{\Lambda^{(n+1)}_{a}\}_{a}  is a quantum instrument.
•
General protocol
We consider the most general case, where  \mathcal{C}  satisfies the following properties:
1.
Completely CP preserving:  (\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathds{1})\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\right]  is completely positive (CP) for all CP maps  \Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1}),\ldots,\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{n}) , where  \mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{n}  and  \mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}  are auxiliary Hilbert spaces and  \mathds{1}  is the identity supermap defined by  \mathcal{\mathds{1}}(\Phi)=\Phi  for all  \Phi:\mathcal{L}\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{i}\right]\to\mathcal{L}\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{i}\right] .
2.
TP preserving:  \mathcal{C}\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\right]  is trace preserving (TP) for all TP maps  \Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{1}),\ldots,\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n}) .
We say that  \mathcal{C}  is a
quantum superchannel
[
42
]
if it satisfies the above conditions. For a probabilistic transformation, we consider the case when  \{\mathcal{C}_{a}\}_{a}  is given by a
quantum superinstrument
, namely,  \mathcal{C}_{a}  is completely CP preserving and  \sum_{a}\mathcal{C}_{a}  is TP preserving. The classes of quantum superchannel and superinstrument include indefinite causal order
[
39
,
9
,
40
]
, which is not realizable in a conventional quantum circuit model.
2.1
Isometry inversion
We consider a probabilistic exact or deterministic implementation of isometry inversion. For a probabilistic exact implementation, we require that the output state is  \mathcal{V}^{-1}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}})  for all  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}({\mathrm{Im}}V)  with probability  p . For a deterministic implementation, we require that the output state is obtained deterministically for all  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}({\mathrm{Im}}V) . We consider the worst-case channel fidelity defined by
\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{worst}}=\inf_{V\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)}F_{\mathrm{ch}}[\mathcal{C}[\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n}]\circ\mathcal{V},\mathds{1}_{d}],
(9)
where  F_{\text{ch}}  is the channel fidelity
[
43
]
and  \mathds{1}_{d}  is the identity operation.
The channel fidelity between a quantum channel  \Lambda  on a  d -dimensional system and a  d -dimensional unitary operation  \mathcal{U}(\cdot)=U\cdot U^{\dagger}  is given by
\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{ch}}(\Lambda,\mathcal{U})={1\over d^{2}}\Tr[J_{\Lambda}|{U}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{U}|],
(10)
where  J_{\Lambda}  and  |{U}\rangle\!\rangle  are the Choi operator of  \Lambda  and the Choi vector of  U
[
44
,
45
]
, respectively, defined by
\displaystyle J_{\Lambda}
\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i,j}\outerproduct{i}{j}\otimes\Lambda(\outerproduct{i}{j}),
(11)
\displaystyle|{U}\rangle\!\rangle
\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i}\ket{i}\otimes U\ket{i}
(12)
using the computational basis  \{\ket{i}\}  of the input system (see Section
3.1.1
for the detail of the Choi representation). Therefore,  F_{\mathrm{ch}}(\Lambda,\mathcal{U})  is linear with respect to  \Lambda . The channel fidelity is invariant under the action of unitary operations, i.e.,
\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{ch}}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\circ\Lambda^{(1)}\circ\mathcal{U},\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\circ\Lambda^{(2)}\circ\mathcal{U})=F_{\mathrm{ch}}(\Lambda^{(1)},\Lambda^{(2)})
(13)
holds for any quantum channels  \Lambda^{(1)}  and  \Lambda^{(2)}  and unitary operations  \mathcal{U}  and  \mathcal{U}^{\prime} . Therefore, when  D=d , the definition (
9
) of the figure of merit for deterministic isometry inversion corresponds to the worst-case channel fidelity for deterministic unitary inversion introduced in Ref.
[
29
]
given by
\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{worst}}=\inf_{U\in\mathbb{U}(d)}F_{\mathrm{ch}}[\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes n}),\mathcal{U}^{-1}].
(14)
We denote the optimal success probability of isometry inversion in parallel, sequential, and general protocols by  p_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)  for  x=\mathrm{PAR}  (parallel),  x=\mathrm{SEQ}  (sequential), and  x=\mathrm{GEN}  (general), respectively. Similarly, we denote the optimal fidelity of isometry inversion by  F_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n) .
2.2
Universal error detection
We define the task called universal error detection to implement the POVM  \{\Pi_{I},\Pi_{O}\}  satisfying the one-sided error condition, i.e.,
\displaystyle\Tr(\Pi_I \rho_\mathrm{in})=1\quad\forall\rho\in\mathcal{L}({\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}).
(15)
We define the figure of merit of universal error detection by the worst-case operational distance given by
\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{worst}}\coloneqq\inf_{V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)}D_{\mathrm{op}}(\{\Pi_{I},\Pi_{O}\},\{\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}\}),
(16)
where  D_{\mathrm{op}}  is the operational distance
[
46
,
47
,
48
]
of two POVMs defined by
\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{op}}(\{\Pi_{i}\}_{i},\{\Pi^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i})\coloneqq\sup_{\rho\geq 0,\Tr\rho=1}{1\over 2}\sum_{i}\absolutevalue{\Tr(\Pi_i \rho) - \Tr(\Pi'_i \rho)},
(17)
which satisfies the convexity and the unitary invariance:
\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{op}}(\{\sum_{j}p_{j}\Pi_{i}^{(j)}\}_{i},\{\sum_{j}p_{j}\Pi_{i}^{\prime(j)}\}_{i})\leq\sum_{j}p_{j}D_{\mathrm{op}}(\{\Pi_{i}^{(j)}\}_{i},\{\Pi_{i}^{\prime(j)}\}_{i})\quad\forall p_{j}\geq 0,
(18)
\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{op}}(\{U\Pi_{i}U^{\dagger}\}_{i},\{U\Pi_{i}^{\prime}U^{\dagger}\}_{i})=D_{\mathrm{op}}(\{\Pi_{i}\}_{i},\{\Pi_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i})\quad\forall U\in\mathbb{U}(D).
(19)
For any distance measure satisfying (
18
) and (
19
), we show that the optimal figure of merit is achieved by the protocol outputting the following POVM (see Theorems
8
and
9
):
\displaystyle\begin{split}\Pi_{I}&=\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}+\alpha(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}),\\
\Pi_{O}&=(1-\alpha)(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}),\end{split}
(20)
and the worst-case operational distance is given by  \delta_{\mathrm{worst}}=\alpha .
We call  \alpha  the approximation error of the universal error detection, and use  \alpha  as the figure of merit in the rest of this paper.
We denote the minimal value of  \alpha  in parallel, sequential, and general protocols as  \alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)  for  x=\mathrm{PAR}  (parallel),  x=\mathrm{SEQ}  (sequential), and  x=\mathrm{GEN}  (general), respectively.
2.3
Isometry adjointation
We demand the quantum superinstrument  \{\mathcal{C}_{I},\mathcal{C}_{O}\}  satisfies
\displaystyle\Tr\mathcal{C}_{I}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}](\rho_{\mathrm{in}})
\displaystyle=1\quad\forall\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{D}({\mathrm{Im}}V),
(21)
which corresponds to the one-sided error condition in universal error detection. We define the quantum channels corresponding to the output quantum instrument and adjoint operation by
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}](\cdot)
\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{I}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}](\cdot)\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}+\mathcal{C}_{O}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}](\cdot)\otimes\outerproduct{1}{1},
(22)
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{adjoint}}(\cdot)
\displaystyle\coloneqq V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}\cdot V_{\mathrm{in}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}+\Tr[(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}})\cdot]{\mathds{1}_{d}\over d}\otimes\outerproduct{1}{1},
(23)
and define the figure of merit by the worst-case diamond-distance error:
\displaystyle\epsilon=\sup_{V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)}{1\over 2}\Big\|\mathcal{C}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}]-\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{adjoint}}\Big\|_{\diamond},
(24)
where  \|\cdot\|_{\diamond}  is the diamond norm
[
49
]
.
We can show that the minimum value of  \epsilon  is achieved by the protocol outputting the following quantum instrument (see Theorems
8
and
9
):
\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{I}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}](\cdot)&=(1-\eta)\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}(\cdot)+\eta{\mathds{1}_{d}\over d}\Tr[\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}\cdot]+\alpha{\mathds{1}_{d}\over d}\Tr[(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}})\cdot],\\
\mathcal{C}_{O}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}](\cdot)&=(1-\alpha){\mathds{1}_{d}\over d}\Tr[(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}})\cdot],\end{split}
(25)
using  \alpha  and  \eta  satisfying  0\leq\alpha,\eta\leq 1 .
We call  \alpha  and  \eta  the detection error and the decode error, respectively.
Using the detection error and the decode error, the diamond-distance error is given by (see Appendix
A.1
)
\displaystyle\epsilon=\max\{\alpha,\eta\}.
(26)
We denote the minimal value of  \epsilon  in parallel, sequential, and general protocols by  \epsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)  for  x=\mathrm{PAR}  (parallel),  x=\mathrm{SEQ}  (sequential), and  x=\mathrm{GEN}  (general), respectively.
3
Construction of isometry adjointation protocols and reduction to isometry inversion and universal error detection
In Section
3.1
, we introduce the Choi representation to represent quantum channels and quantum supermaps. We also introduce the link product to represent their compositions.
Then, we introduce the group theoretic technique called the Schur-Weyl duality.
In Section
3.2
, we construct isometry adjointation protocols by converting unitary inversion protocols using the quantum comb derived from the Schur-Weyl duality.
We also derive isometry inversion and universal error detection protocols from the isometry adjointation protocol.
In the following discussions, we suppose  \mathcal{I}_{1}=\cdots\mathcal{I}_{n}=\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{1}=\cdots=\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}^{d} ,  \mathcal{O}_{1}=\cdots=\mathcal{O}_{n}=\mathcal{P}=\mathbb{C}^{D} , and denote the joint Hilbert spaces by  \mathcal{I}^{i}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i}\mathcal{I}_{j} ,  \mathcal{O}^{\prime i}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{j} , and  \mathcal{O}^{i}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{i}\mathcal{O}_{j}  for  i\in\{1,\ldots,n\} .
To illustrate the dimensions of the Hilbert spaces corresponding to the wires in the quantum circuits shown in this Section, we utilize the following color code of wires: a red wire corresponds to a  d -dimensional Hilbert space, a blue wire corresponds to a  D -dimensional Hilbert space, and a black wire corresponds to a Hilbert space with an arbitrary dimension.
The dual wires in the quantum circuits represent classical information transmissions.
3.1
Preliminaries
3.1.1
Choi representation and link product
Any quantum channel  \Lambda:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O})  can be represented by the Choi operator  J_{\Lambda}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{O})  defined by
[
44
,
45
]
\displaystyle J_{\Lambda}\coloneqq\sum_{i,j}\outerproduct{i}{j}_{\mathcal{I}}\otimes\Lambda(\outerproduct{i}{j})_{\mathcal{O}},
(27)
where  \{\ket{i}\}  is the computational basis of  \mathcal{I} . In particular, the Choi operator  J_{\mathcal{V}}  of an isometry operation  \mathcal{V}(\cdot)\coloneqq V\cdot V^{\dagger}  for an isometry operator  V:\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{O}  is represented as
\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{V}}=|{V}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{V}|,
(28)
where  |{V}\rangle\!\rangle\in\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{O}  is the Choi vector of  V  defined by
\displaystyle|{V}\rangle\!\rangle\coloneqq\sum_{i}\ket{i}_{\mathcal{I}}\otimes(V\ket{i})_{\mathcal{O}}.
(29)
A composition of two quantum channels  \Lambda^{(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{1})  and  \Lambda^{(2)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{2})  can be represented in terms of the corresponding Choi operators as
\displaystyle J_{\Lambda^{(2)}\circ\Lambda^{(1)}}=J_{\Lambda^{(2)}}\star J_{\Lambda^{(1)}},
(30)
where  \star  is the link product
[
50
]
defined by
\displaystyle A\star B\coloneqq\Tr_{\mathcal{B}}[(A^{T_{\mathcal{B}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{C}})(\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes B)]
(31)
for  A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})  and  B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}) , and  A^{T_{\mathcal{B}}}  is the partial transpose of  A  with respect to the subsystem  \mathcal{B} . The link product satisfies the commutativity and associativity relations by definition, given as
\displaystyle A\star B
\displaystyle=B\star A,
(32)
\displaystyle(A\star B)\star C
\displaystyle=A\star(B\star C)
(33)
for  A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}) ,  B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}) , and  C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D}) . If the two operators  A  and  B  does not have an overlap subsystem, i.e.,  A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})  and  B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B})  for  \mathcal{A}\neq\mathcal{B} , the link product of  A  and  B  becomes the tensor product:
\displaystyle A\star B=A\otimes B.
(34)
Using the Choi operator and the link product, we can represent the quantum combs defined in Eq. (
7
).
The Choi operator of the quantum channel shown in the right-hand side of Eq. (
7
) is given by
\displaystyle J_{\Lambda^{(n+1)}}\star J_{\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}}\star J_{\Lambda^{(n)}}\star\cdots\star J_{\Lambda^{(2)}}\star J_{\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}}\star J_{\Lambda^{(1)}}.
(35)
Using Eqs. (
32
)-(
34
), this can be rewritten as
\displaystyle C\star\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}J_{\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(i)}},
(36)
where  C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{F})  is the Choi operator of the quantum comb  \mathcal{C}  defined by
\displaystyle C\coloneqq J_{\Lambda^{(n+1)}}\star J_{\Lambda^{(n)}}\star\cdots\star J_{\Lambda^{(1)}}.
(37)
Therefore, the action of a quantum comb  \mathcal{C}  on input quantum operations  \{\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)},\ldots,\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}\}  is given in the Choi representation by
\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{C}[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}]}=C\star\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}J_{\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(i)}}.
(38)
The following Theorem characterizes the set of Choi operators of quantum combs.
Theorem 1
.
[
51
,
50
]
Suppose  \mathcal{P},\mathcal{F},\mathcal{I}_{i},\mathcal{O}_{i}  for  i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}  are Hilbert spaces and define the joint Hilbert spaces by  \mathcal{I}^{n}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{I}_{i}  and  \mathcal{O}^{n}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{i} . The operator  C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{F})  can be written as Eq. (
37
) using quantum channels  \Lambda^{(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{1}),\Lambda^{(2)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{2}),\ldots,\Lambda^{(n)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n-1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n}),\Lambda^{(n+1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})  and auxiliary Hilbert spaces  \mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{n}  if and only if  C  satisfies the following equations:
\displaystyle C\geq 0,
(39)
\displaystyle\Tr_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}C^{(i)}=C^{(i-1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{i-1}}\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\},
(40)
where  \mathcal{O}_{0}  and  \mathcal{I}_{n+1}  are defined by  \mathcal{O}_{0}\coloneqq\mathcal{P}  and  \mathcal{I}_{n+1}\coloneqq\mathcal{F} , and  C^{(i)}  for  i\in\{0,\ldots,n+1\}  are defined by  C^{(n+1)}\coloneqq C ,  C^{(i-1)}\coloneqq\Tr_{\mathcal{O}_{i-1}\mathcal{I}_{i}}C^{(i)}/\dim\mathcal{O}_{i-1}  and  C^{(0)}\coloneqq 1 .
The probabilistic transformation  \{\mathcal{C}_{a}\}_{a}  in a sequential protocol given by Eq. (
8
) can be similarly represented by
\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{C}_{a}[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}]}=C_{a}\star\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}J_{\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(i)}},
(41)
where  C_{a}  is the Choi operator of the probabilistic transformation  \{\mathcal{C}_{a}\}_{a}  defined by
\displaystyle C_{a}\coloneqq J_{\Lambda^{(n+1)}_{a}}\star J_{\Lambda^{(n)}}\star\cdots\star J_{\Lambda^{(1)}}.
(42)
The following Theorem characterizes the set of Choi operators of probabilistic transformations implemented in sequential protocols.
Theorem 2
.
[
51
,
50
]
Suppose  \mathcal{P},\mathcal{F},\mathcal{I}_{i},\mathcal{O}_{i}  for  i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}  are Hilbert spaces and define the joint Hilbert spaces by  \mathcal{I}^{n}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{I}_{i}  and  \mathcal{O}^{n}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{i} . The set of operators  \{C_{a}\}_{a}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{F})  can be written as Eq. (
37
) using quantum channels  \Lambda^{(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{1}),\Lambda^{(2)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{2}),\ldots,\Lambda^{(n)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n-1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n}) , a quantum instrument  \{\Lambda^{(n+1)}_{a}\}_{a}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})  and auxiliary Hilbert spaces  \mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{n}  if and only if  C  satisfies the following equations:
\displaystyle C_{a}\geq 0,
(43)
\displaystyle\Tr_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}C^{(i)}=C^{(i-1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{i-1}}\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\},
(44)
where  \mathcal{O}_{0}  and  \mathcal{I}_{n+1}  are defined by  \mathcal{O}_{0}\coloneqq\mathcal{P}  and  \mathcal{I}_{n+1}\coloneqq\mathcal{F} , and  C^{(i)}  for  i\in\{0,\ldots,n+1\}  are defined by  C^{(n+1)}\coloneqq\sum_{a}C_{a} ,  C^{(i-1)}\coloneqq\Tr_{\mathcal{O}_{i-1}\mathcal{I}_{i}}C^{(i)}/\dim\mathcal{O}_{i-1}  and  C^{(0)}\coloneqq 1 .
The link product also represents the composition of two quantum combs.
We consider two quantum combs  \mathcal{C}^{\prime}  and  \mathcal{T}  defined by
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\left[\Phi_{\text{in}}^{\prime(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\text{in}}^{\prime(n)}\right]
\displaystyle\coloneqq\Lambda^{\prime(n+1)}\circ(\Phi_{\text{in}}^{\prime(n)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{n}})\circ\cdots\circ\Lambda^{\prime(2)}\circ(\Phi_{\text{in}}^{\prime(1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}})\circ\Lambda^{\prime(1)},
(45)
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\left[\Phi_{\text{in}}^{\prime\prime(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\text{in}}^{\prime\prime(n)}\right]
\displaystyle\coloneqq\Gamma^{(n+1)}\circ(\Phi_{\text{in}}^{\prime\prime(n)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{n}})\circ\cdots\circ\Gamma^{(2)}\circ(\Phi_{\text{in}}^{\prime\prime(1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}})\circ\Gamma^{(1)},
(46)
where  \mathcal{A}_{i}  and  \mathcal{B}_{i}  for  i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}  are auxiliary Hilbert spaces and  \Lambda^{\prime(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{1}) ,  \Lambda^{\prime(i)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i-1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{i-1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{i})  for  i\in\{2,\ldots,n\} ,  \Lambda^{(n+1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}) ,  \Gamma^{(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1}) ,  \Gamma^{(i)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{i-1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{i-1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i-1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{i})  for  i\in\{2,\ldots,n\} , and  \Gamma^{(n+1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n})  are quantum channels.
We define the composed quantum comb  \mathcal{C}  by
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\left[\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(1)},\ldots,\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(n)}\right]
\displaystyle\coloneqq\Lambda^{(n+1)}\circ(\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(n)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{n}\mathcal{B}_{n}})\circ\cdots\circ\Lambda^{(2)}\circ(\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathcal{B}_{1}})\circ\Lambda^{(1)},
(47)
\displaystyle\Lambda^{(i)}
\displaystyle\coloneqq(\Lambda^{\prime(i)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}})\circ(\Gamma^{(i)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}})\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}.
(48)
In terms of the Choi operator, this composition can be written as
\displaystyle C=C^{\prime}\star T,
(49)
where  C^{\prime} ,  T , and  C  are Choi operators of  \mathcal{C}^{\prime} ,  \mathcal{T} , and  \mathcal{C} , respectively.
3.1.2
Schur-Weyl duality
We introduce the Schur-Weyl duality as follows. We consider representations of the special unitary group  \mathbb{U}(d)  and the permutation group  \mathfrak{S}_{n}  on a  n -fold Hilbert space  (\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}  defined by
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}(d)\ni U
\displaystyle\mapsto U^{\otimes n}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n},
(50)
\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{n}\ni\pi
\displaystyle\mapsto P_{\pi}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n},
(51)
where  P_{\pi}  is a permutation operator on  (\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}  defined by
\displaystyle P_{\pi}(\ket{\psi_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{\psi_n})\coloneqq\ket{\psi_{\pi^{-1}(1)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{\psi_{\pi^{-1}(n)}}\quad\forall\ket{\psi_1},\ldots,\ket{\psi_n}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}.
(52)
The Schur-Weyl duality asserts a simultaneous irreducible decomposition of the two representations  U^{\otimes n}  and  P_{\pi}  given by
\displaystyle(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}\otimes\mathcal{S}_{\mu},
(53)
\displaystyle U^{\otimes n}
\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}(U_{\mu})_{\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}},
(54)
\displaystyle P_{\pi}
\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}}\otimes(\pi_{\mu})_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}},
(55)
where  \mu  runs in the set of Young diagrams with  n  boxes whose depth is less than or equal to  d , denoted by  \mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n} , and  U_{\mu}  and  \pi_{\mu}  are irreducible representations of  \mathbb{U}(d)  and  \mathfrak{S}_{n}  on the linear spaces  \mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}  and  \mathcal{S}_{\mu} , respectively.
5
5
5
The superscript  d  is put on  \mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}  since the properties of the representation space  \mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)} , e.g., the dimension, depends on the local dimension  d . On the other hand, we do not put the superscript  d  on  \mathcal{S}_{\mu}  like  \mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(d)}  since the representation space  \mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(d)}  is automorphic to  \mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(d^{\prime})}  for an arbitrary local dimension  d^{\prime} .
We denote the dimensions of the irreducible representation spaces  \mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}  and  \mathcal{S}_{\mu}  by  m_{\mu}^{(d)}  and  d_{\mu} , respectively.
6
6
6
The dimension of  \mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}  is denoted by  m_{\mu}^{(d)}  since  \mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}  is the multiplicity space corresponding to the irreducible representation space  \mathcal{S}_{\mu}  [see Eq. (
53
)].
They are given by
[
52
]
\displaystyle d_{\mu}
\displaystyle={n!\over\mathrm{hook}(\mu)},
(56)
\displaystyle m_{\mu}^{(d)}
\displaystyle={\prod_{(i,j)\in\mu}(d+j-i)\over\text{hook}(\mu)},
(57)
where  \mathrm{hook}(\mu)  for  \mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}  is defined by
\displaystyle\mathrm{hook}(\mu)=\prod_{(i,j)\in\mu}(\mu_{i}+\mu^{\prime}_{j}-i-j+1),
(58)
and  (i,j)  is the coordinate of a box in the Young diagram  \mu  such that  i  represents the row number running from bottom to top and  j  represents the column number running from left to right. The numbers  \mu_{i}  and  \mu^{\prime}_{j}  are the numbers of boxes in the  i -th row and the  j -th column, respectively.
The  n -fold isometry operator  V^{\otimes n}  for  V\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)  can also be decomposed as
\displaystyle V^{\otimes n}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}(V_{\mu})_{\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}\to\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(D)}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}},
(59)
where  V_{\mu}:\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}\to\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(D)}  is an isometry operator, as shown in Ref.
[
36
]
.
Due to Schur’s lemma, any operator commuting with  U^{\otimes n}  for all  U\in\mathbb{U}(d)  can be written as a linear combination of the operators  E^{\mu,d}_{ij}  defined by
\displaystyle E^{\mu,d}_{ij}\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}}\otimes\outerproduct{\mu, i}{\mu, j}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}}
(60)
for  i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d_{\mu}\} , where  \{\ket{\mu, i}\}  is an orthonormal basis of  \mathcal{S}_{\mu} . Then, the following relation holds:
\displaystyle\Tr E^{\mu,d}_{ij}=m_{\mu}^{(d)}\delta_{ij},\;\;\;E^{\mu,d}_{ij}E^{\nu,d}_{kl}=\delta_{\mu\nu}\delta_{jk}E^{\mu,d}_{il},
(61)
where  \delta_{ij}  is Kronecker’s delta defined by  \delta_{ii}=1  and  \delta_{ij}=0  for  i\neq j .
In particular,  \{m_{\mu}^{(d)-1/2}E^{\mu,d}_{ij}\}  forms an orthonormal basis of the set of operators commuting with  U^{\otimes n}  for all  U\in\mathbb{U}(d)  under the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product  \langle X,Y\rangle\coloneqq\Tr(X^\dagger Y) . Thus, any operator  \rho  commuting with  U^{\otimes n}  for all  U\in\mathbb{U}(d)  can be represented as
\displaystyle\rho=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d_{\mu}}{\Tr(E^{\mu,d}_{ji} \rho)\over m_{\mu}^{(d)}}E^{\mu,d}_{ij}.
(62)
Also, we define the Young projector  \Pi_{\mu}  by
\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu}^{(d)}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{d_{\mu}}E^{\mu,d}_{ii},
(63)
which is an orthonormal projector onto the subspace  \mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}\otimes\mathcal{S}_{\mu} .
In particular, we consider the Schur basis of  (\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}  defined by
\displaystyle\ket{\mu, u, i}\coloneqq\ket{\mu,u}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}}\otimes\ket{\mu,i}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}},
(64)
where  \{\ket{\mu, u}\}  is the Gelfand-Zetlin basis of  \mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}  and  \{\ket{\mu,i}\}  is the Young-Yamanouchi basis of  \mathcal{S}_{\mu} .
The change of the basis from the computational basis to the Schur basis is called the quantum Schur transform
[
53
,
54
,
55
,
56
,
57
,
58
]
, denoted by  \mathrm{Sch}_{n,d} .
In the quantum circuit, the Schur basis is represented in three registers corresponding to Hilbert spaces  \mathcal{T}_{n} ,  \mathcal{U}_{n}^{(d)}  and  \mathcal{S}_{n} , as
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sch}_{n,d}\ket{\mu,u,i}=\ket{\mu}_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}\otimes\ket{u}_{\mathcal{U}_{n}^{(d)}}\otimes\ket{i}_{\mathcal{S}_{n}},
(65)
such that the spaces  \mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}  and  \mathcal{S}_{\mu}  can be embedded into  \mathcal{U}_{n}^{(d)}  and  \mathcal{S}_{n} , respectively.
We call  \mathcal{T}_{n} ,  \mathcal{U}_{n}^{(d)}  and  \mathcal{S}_{n}  the Young diagram register, the unitary group register, and the symmetric group register, respectively.
The standard tableaux with frame  \mu  is indexed by  i\in\{1,\ldots,d_{\mu}\}  and the  i -th standard tableau is denoted by  s^{\mu}_{i} . Each element in the Young-Yamanouchi basis  \{\ket{\mu, i}\}  is associated with the standard tableaux  s^{\mu}_{i} . We also define the set of operators  \{E^{\lambda,d}_{ab}\}  on  (\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n-1}  for  \lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n-1}  and  a,b\in\{1,\ldots,d_{\lambda}\} .
To express the relation between  \{E^{\mu,d}_{ij}\}  and  \{E^{\lambda,d}_{ab}\} , we introduce the following notations on the Young diagrams. We denote the set of Young diagrams obtained by adding (removing) a box to  \lambda  by  \lambda+\square  ( \lambda-\square ), and define the set  \lambda+_{d}\square  by
\displaystyle\lambda+_{d}\square\coloneqq\{\mu\in\lambda+\square\mid(\text{depth of }\mu)\leq d\}.
(66)
We also denote the index of the standard tableau  s^{\mu}_{a_{\mu}^{\lambda}}  obtained by adding a box  n  to a standard tableau  s^{\lambda}_{a}  by  a_{\mu}^{\lambda} .

The tensor product $E^{\lambda,d}_{ab}\otimes\mathds{1}_{d}$ and the partial trace of $E^{\mu,d}_{ij}$ in the last system for $\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n-1}$ and $\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}$ are given by
\@@amsalign
\end{Lem}\par

The tensor product $E^{\lambda,d}_{ab}\otimes\mathds{1}_{d}$ and the partial trace of $E^{\mu,d}_{ij}$ in the last system for $\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n-1}$ and $\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}$ are given by

\end{Lem}\par
3.2
Construction of isometry adjointation protocol
3.2.1
Conversion from unitary inversion to isometry adjointation
In this section, we derive a probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{I},\mathcal{T}_{O}\}  converting a unitary inversion protocol to an isometry adjointation protocol as
\displaystyle C^{\prime}\star|{U_{\mathrm{in}}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{U_{\mathrm{in}}}|^{\otimes n}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}}\approx|{U_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{U_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}}|_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mathcal{F}}\quad\forall U_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{U}(d)
\displaystyle\Longrightarrow T_{I}\star C^{\prime}\star|{V_{\mathrm{in}}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{V_{\mathrm{in}}}|^{\otimes n}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathcal{O}^{n}}\approx|{V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{F}}\quad\forall V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D),
(70)
where  C^{\prime}  is the Choi operator corresponding to a quantum comb implementing unitary inversion, and  T_{I}  is the Choi operator corresponding to  \mathcal{T}_{I} .
The action of  T_{I}  is to “compress” the sequentially given quantum states in  \mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}=(\mathbb{C}^{D})^{\otimes n+1}  into  \mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}=(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n+1}  [see also the red quantum comb in Fig.
3
(b)].
†
†
itembox-title:
(a) Unitary inversion
[1] Deterministic protocol
[2] Probabilistic exact protocol
\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad\begin{minipage}[324.30084pt]\begin{itembox}[(b) Isometry adjointation]\centering\par\noindent\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\ignorespaces\includegraphics[]{sequential_isometry_adjointation.pdf}
}}}}
\@add@centering\end{itembox}\end{minipage}
\displaystyle\rightarrow\begin{cases}\begin{minipage}[324.30084pt]\begin{itembox}[(c) Isometry inversion][1] Deterministic protocol\\
\noindent\par\noindent\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\ignorespaces\includegraphics[]{sequential_deterministic_isometry_inversion.pdf}
}}}}\\
\hrule\quad\\
 Probabilistic exact protocol\\
\noindent\par\noindent\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\ignorespaces\includegraphics[]{sequential_probabilistic_isometry_inversion.pdf}
}}}}
\end{itembox}\end{minipage}\\
\quad\\
\begin{minipage}[324.30084pt]\begin{itembox}[(d) Universal error detection]\centering\par\noindent\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\ignorespaces\includegraphics[]{sequential_universal_error_detection.pdf}
}}}}
\@add@centering\end{itembox}\end{minipage}\end{cases}
Figure 3:
(a) Sequential protocols for deterministic and probabilistic exact unitary inversion. (b) A sequential isometry adjointation protocol is constructed by converting the unitary inversion protocol using a probabilistic quantum comb. (c, d) Reduction to isometry inversion and universal error detection by discarding the measurement outcome and the output state of the isometry adjointation protocol, respectively.
We define  T_{I}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n})  using the operators  \{E^{\mu,d}_{ij}\}  introduced in Section
3.1.2
by
\displaystyle T_{I}
\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n+1}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d_{\mu}}{(E^{\mu,d}_{ij})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}}\otimes(E^{\mu,D}_{ij})_{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{O}^{n}}\over{m_{\mu}^{(d)}}}.
(71)
To understand the action of  T_{I} , we consider the link product
\displaystyle\psi_{\mathrm{out}}\coloneqq T_{I}\star\outerproduct{\psi_\mathrm{in}}{\psi_\mathrm{in}}
(72)
for  \ket{\psi_\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n} .
Suppose  \ket{\psi_\mathrm{in}}  is expressed in the Schur basis as
\displaystyle\ket{\psi_\mathrm{in}}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{D}_{n+1}}\sum_{u,i}a_{\mu,u,i}\ket{\mu,u}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(D)}}\otimes\ket{\mu,i}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}}.
(73)
Then,  \psi_{\mathrm{out}}  is given by
\displaystyle\psi_{\mathrm{out}}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n+1}}\sum_{u,i,i^{\prime}}a_{\mu,u,i}a_{\mu,u,i^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}}\over m_{\mu}^{(d)}}\otimes\outerproduct{\mu, i}{\mu,i'}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}}.
(74)
From this observation, the action of  T_{I}  is understood to be the following two actions:
•
Detect whether the state in  \mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}=(\mathbb{C}^{D})^{\otimes n+1}  is inside  \bigoplus_{V\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)}{\mathrm{Im}}V^{\otimes n+1}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n+1}}\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(D)}\otimes\mathcal{S}_{\mu}  by checking the depth of  \mu .
•
Discard the state in  \mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(D)}  to compress the state in  \mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}=(\mathbb{C}^{D})^{\otimes n+1}  into  \mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}=(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n+1} .
We can use these two properties to implement isometry adjointation, which is discussed more formally in Theorem
4
.
As shown in Fig.
4
, we construct a probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{I},\mathcal{T}_{O}\}  such that the Choi operator of  \mathcal{T}_{I}  is given by Eq. (
71
), i.e., we construct quantum channels  \Gamma^{(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1}),\Gamma^{(2)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}),\ldots,\Gamma^{(n)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{n-1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{n}) , a quantum instrument  \{\Gamma^{(n+1)}_{I},\Gamma^{(n+1)}_{O}\}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})  and auxiliary Hilbert spaces  \mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}  such that
\displaystyle T_{a}=J_{\Gamma^{(n+1)}_{a}}\star J_{\Gamma^{(n)}}\star\cdots\star J_{\Gamma^{(1)}}\quad\forall a\in\{I,O\},
(75)
where  J_{\Gamma}  is the Choi operator of  \Gamma\in\{\Gamma^{(1)},\ldots,\Gamma^{(n)},\Gamma_{a}^{(n+1)}\} .
The construction of the probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{I},\mathcal{T}_{O}\}  is based on the (dual) Clebsch-Gordan (CG) transforms (see Appendix
A.2
for the definition).
Figure 4:
The construction of the probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{I},\mathcal{T}_{O}\} . The open wires  \mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n}  correspond to the blue and red wires shown in Fig.
3
(b), and the double wire corresponds to the classical 1-bit register  \mathcal{C} . This figure corresponds to  \mathcal{T}_{I} . If the measurement outcome of the quantum instrument  \mathcal{P}_{t_{i}}  is 1 for  i\in\{2,\ldots,n+1\} , we output the maximally mixed state  \mathds{1}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i-1}}/d , and the rest of the quantum channels  \Gamma^{(i+1)},\ldots,\Gamma^{(n+1)}  becomes trace-and-replace channels. Otherwise, we apply the quantum channel  \Gamma^{(i+1)}  shown in this figure [see also Eq. (
79
)].
We define the Hilbert spaces  \mathcal{B}_{i}  for  i\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}  by
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{1}
\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(d)}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(D)}\otimes\mathcal{C},
(76)
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i}
\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(d)}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(D)}\otimes\mathcal{C}\quad\forall i\geq 2,
(77)
where  \mathcal{C}  is a 1-bit classical register, i.e.,  \mathcal{C}=\mathrm{span}\{\outerproduct{0}{0},\outerproduct{1}{1}\} .
The quantum channel  \Gamma^{(1)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{1})  is given by
\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}(\cdot)\coloneqq(\phi^{+}_{d})_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(d)}}\otimes(\cdot)_{\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(D)}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}_{\mathcal{C}},
(78)
where  \phi^{+}_{d}  is defined by  \phi^{+}_{d}=\outerproduct{\phi^+_d}{\phi^+_d} .
The quantum channel  \Gamma^{(i)}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{i-1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{i-1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i-1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{i})  for  i\in\{2,\ldots,n+1\}  is given by
\displaystyle\Gamma^{(i)}(\rho_{\mathcal{O}_{i-1}\mathcal{U}_{i-1}^{(d)}\mathcal{T}_{i-1}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(D)}}\otimes\outerproduct{s}{s}_{\mathcal{C}})
\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\sum_{t_{i}=0}^{1}(\mathcal{D}^{(t_{i})})_{\mathcal{U}_{i-1}^{(d)}\mathcal{T}_{i-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(D)}\to\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i-1}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(d)}\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(D)}}\circ[(\mathcal{P}_{t_{i}})_{\mathcal{T}_{i}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{i-1}^{(d)}\mathcal{T}_{i-1}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(D)}}]\\
\hskip 30.0pt\circ[(\mathcal{CG}^{+}_{i,D})_{\mathcal{T}_{i-1}\mathcal{U}_{i-1}^{(D)}\mathcal{O}_{i-1}\to\mathcal{T}_{i-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(D)}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{i-1}^{(d)}}](\rho)\otimes\outerproduct{t_i}{t_i}_{\mathcal{C}}&(s=0)\\
{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i}}\over D}\Tr(\rho)\otimes\mathrm{garbage}_{\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(d)}\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(D)}}\otimes\outerproduct{1}{1}_{\mathcal{C}}&(s=1)\end{cases},
(79)
where  \mathcal{CG}^{+}_{i,D}  is the isometry operation defined by
\displaystyle\mathcal{CG}^{+}_{i,D}(\cdot)\coloneqq\mathrm{CG}^{+}_{i,D}\cdot\left(\mathrm{CG}^{+}_{i,D}\right)^{\dagger}
(80)
using the CG transform  \mathrm{CG}^{+}_{i,D}  defined in Eq. (
169
),  \{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t=0}^{1}  is the quantum instrument defined by
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{t}\coloneqq P_{t}\cdot P_{t},\quad P_{0}\coloneqq\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{i}}\outerproduct{\mu}{\mu}_{\mathcal{T}_{i}},\quad P_{1}\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{i}}-P_{0},
(81)
\mathcal{D}^{(t)}  is the quantum channel conditioned on the measurement outcome  t  of the quantum instrument  \{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t=0}^{1}  defined by
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(t)}(\cdot)\coloneqq\begin{cases}[(\mathcal{CG}^{-}_{i,d})^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{U}_{i-1}^{(d)}\mathcal{T}_{i-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}\to\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i-1}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(d)}\mathcal{T}_{i}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(D)}}](\cdot)&(t=0)\\
{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i}}\over D}\Tr(\cdot)\otimes\mathrm{garbage}_{\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(d)}\mathcal{T}_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(D)}}&(t=1)\end{cases},
(82)
\mathcal{CG}^{-}_{i,d}  is the isometry operation defined by
\displaystyle\mathcal{CG}^{-}_{i,d}(\cdot)\coloneqq\mathrm{CG}^{-}_{i,d}\cdot\left(\mathrm{CG}^{-}_{i,d}\right)^{\dagger}
(83)
using the dual CG transform  \mathrm{CG}^{-}_{i,d}  defined in Eq. (
A.2
), and  \mathrm{garbage}  is an arbitrary quantum state.
In the quantum circuit shown in Fig.
4
, the (dual) CG transforms are represented as controlled operations since they can be written as controlled isometry operations as shown in Eqs. (
170
) and (
A.2
).
Note that  \outerproduct{\square}{\square}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}  is omitted in Eq. (
79
) for  i=2  since  \mathcal{T}_{1}=\mathbb{C}  holds.
The quantum instrument  \{\Gamma^{(n+1)}_{I},\Gamma^{(n+1)}_{O}\}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n})  is defined by
\displaystyle\Gamma^{(n+1)}_{a_{s}}(\cdot)\coloneqq\Tr_{\mathcal{U}_{n+1}^{(d)}\mathcal{T}_{n+1}\mathcal{U}_{n+1}^{(D)}\mathcal{C}}[\Gamma^{(n+1)}(\cdot)(\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n}\mathcal{U}_{n+1}^{(d)}\mathcal{T}_{n+1}\mathcal{U}_{n+1}^{(D)}}\otimes\outerproduct{s}{s}_{\mathcal{C}})]\quad\forall s\in\{0,1\}
(84)
for  a_{0}=I  and  a_{1}=O .
In Appendix
A.3
, we show that this construction gives the Choi operator  T_{I}  given in Eq. (
71
).
The probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{I},\mathcal{T}_{O}\}  can be efficiently implementable by using efficient implementations of (dual) CG transforms
[
53
,
54
,
62
,
63
,
64
]
.
The bottleneck of the quantum circuit shown in Fig.
4
is a sequence of (dual) CG transforms, which can be implemented with the precision  \epsilon^{\prime}  with the circuit complexity  O(\mathrm{poly}(n,D,\log\epsilon^{\prime-1})) .
We compose the probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{I},\mathcal{T}_{O}\}  with the unitary inversion protocol to implement isometry adjointation.
Suppose a quantum comb  \mathcal{C}^{\prime}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}[\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{i})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{i})]\to[\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})]  given by [see Fig.
3
(a-1)]
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\left[\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(1)},\ldots,\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(n)}\right]
\displaystyle\coloneqq\Lambda^{\prime(n+1)}\circ(\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(n)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{n}})\circ\cdots\circ\Lambda^{\prime(2)}\circ(\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}})\circ\Lambda^{\prime(1)}
(85)
implements deterministic unitary inversion approximately:
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n})\approx\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}\quad\forall U_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{U}(d).
(86)
Reference
[
29
]
shows that the optimal worst-case channel fidelity of unitary inversion is achieved with the protocol whose Choi operator  C^{\prime}  satisfies the  \mathbb{U}(d)\times\mathbb{U}(d)  symmetry given by
\displaystyle[C^{\prime},U^{\prime\otimes n+1}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathcal{F}}\otimes U^{\prime\prime\otimes n+1}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}}]=0\quad\forall U^{\prime},U^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{U}(d).
(87)
From the sequential unitary inversion protocol satisfying the  \mathbb{U}(d)\times\mathbb{U}(d)  symmetry (
87
), we define a probabilistic transformation as follows [see Fig.
3
(b)]:
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{a}\left[\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(1)},\ldots,\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(n)}\right]
\displaystyle\coloneqq\Lambda^{(n+1)}_{a}\circ(\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(n)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{n}\mathcal{B}_{n}})\circ\cdots\circ\Lambda^{(2)}\circ(\Phi_{\text{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathcal{B}_{1}})\circ\Lambda^{(1)},
(88)
\displaystyle\Lambda^{(i)}
\displaystyle\coloneqq(\Lambda^{\prime(i)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}})\circ(\Gamma^{(i)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{i-1}})\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},
(89)
\displaystyle\Lambda^{(n+1)}_{a}
\displaystyle\coloneqq(\Lambda^{\prime(n+1)}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{n+1}})\circ(\Gamma^{(n+1)}_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}),
(90)
where  \Gamma^{(1)},\ldots,\Gamma^{(n)}  and  \{\Gamma^{(n+1)}_{a}\}_{a}  are quantum channels and a quantum instrument composing the probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{a}\}_{a}  and  \Lambda^{\prime(1)},\ldots,\Lambda^{\prime(n+1)}  are quantum channels composing the unitary inversion quantum comb  \mathcal{C}^{\prime}  [see Eqs. (
75
) and (
85
)].
The supermap  \{\mathcal{C}_{a}\}_{a}  implements isometry adjointation as shown in the following Theorem.
Theorem 4
.
The sequential protocol shown in Fig.
3
(b) implements an isometry adjointation the detection error  \alpha=\alpha_{C^{\prime}}  and the decode error  \eta=1-F_{\mathrm{UI}}  in Eq. (
25
), where  \alpha_{C^{\prime}}  is defined by
\displaystyle\alpha_{C^{\prime}}\coloneqq\Tr[\Tr_{\mathcal{F}}(C^{\prime})\Sigma],
(91)
\Sigma
is defined by
\displaystyle\Sigma\coloneqq\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}\sum_{\mu\in\lambda+_{d}\square}\sum_{a,b=1}^{d_{\lambda}}{\mathrm{hook}(\lambda)\over\mathrm{hook}(\mu)}(E^{\lambda,d}_{ab})_{\mathcal{I}^{n}}\otimes{(E^{\mu,d}_{a^{\lambda}_{\mu}b^{\lambda}_{\mu}})_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\over m_{\mu}^{(d)}},
(92)
and  F_{\mathrm{UI}}  is the worst-case channel fidelity of the unitary inversion.
Thus, the worst-case diamond-norm error is given by
\displaystyle\epsilon=\max\{\alpha_{C^{\prime}},1-F_{\mathrm{UI}}\}.
(93)
Proof sketch.
We show that the probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{I},\mathcal{T}_{O}\}  derived in this section satisfies
\displaystyle T_{I}\star|{V_{\mathrm{in}}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{V_{\mathrm{in}}}|^{\otimes n}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathcal{O}^{n}}\approx\int_{\mathbb{U}(d)}\differential U|{UV_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{UV_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\otimes|{U}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{U}|^{\otimes n}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}},
(94)
where  \differential U  is the Haar measure of  \mathbb{U}(d) .
Then, Eq. (
70
) holds since
\displaystyle T_{I}\star C^{\prime}\star|{V_{\mathrm{in}}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{V_{\mathrm{in}}}|^{\otimes n}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathcal{O}^{n}}
\displaystyle\approx C^{\prime}\star\int_{\mathbb{U}(d)}\differential U|{UV_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{UV_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\otimes|{U}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{U}|^{\otimes n}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}}
(95)
\displaystyle\approx\int_{\mathbb{U}(d)}\differential U|{UV_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{UV_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\star|{U^{-1}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{U^{-1}}|_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mathcal{F}}
(96)
\displaystyle=|{V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{F}}.
(97)
See Appendix
A.4
for the detail.

3.2.2
Asymptotically optimal parallel protocol for isometry adjointation
We construct an asymptotically optimal parallel protocol for isometry adjointation by converting the parallel protocol for unitary inversion.
The optimal parallel protocol for deterministic unitary inversion is investigated in Ref.
[
29
]
, which shows that the estimation-based protocol achieves the optimal worst-case channel fidelity among all parallel protocols.
In the estimation-based unitary inversion protocol, one first estimates the input unitary operation  U_{\mathrm{in}}  by applying  U_{\mathrm{in}}  in parallel to a quantum state  \phi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{A}) , and measure the output state by a POVM  \{M_{i}\}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}\otimes\mathcal{A}) , where  \mathcal{A}  is an auxiliary Hilbert space. We define the measurement channel  \{\mathcal{M}_{i}\}_{i}  corresponding to the POVM  \{M_{i}\}  defined by  \mathcal{M}_{i}(\cdot)\coloneqq\Tr(M_i \cdot) . Then, one calculates the inverse operation  \mathcal{R}_{i}  of the estimated unitary operation and applies  \mathcal{R}_{i}  on the input quantum state  \rho_{\mathrm{in}} . This protocol can be expressed as [see Fig.
5
(a-1)]
\displaystyle\sum_{i}(\mathcal{R}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{i})[\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\otimes(\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}})(\phi)]\approx\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}})
(98)
for all  U_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{U}(d)  and  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d}) .
The worst-case channel fidelity of the unitary inversion is the same as the entanglement fidelity of the unitary estimation protocol given by
\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{est}}\coloneqq\inf_{U_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{U}(d)}F_{\mathrm{ch}}[\mathcal{E}_{U_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{in}}].
(99)
Here,  \mathcal{E}_{U_{\mathrm{in}}}  is the measure-and-prepare channel defined by
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{U_{\mathrm{in}}}\coloneqq\sum_{i}p(\hat{U}_{i}|U_{\mathrm{in}})\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{i},
(100)
where  p(\hat{U}_{i}|U_{\mathrm{in}})  is the probability to estimate the input unitary operation  U_{\mathrm{in}}  as  \hat{U}_{i} .
The optimal estimation is shown to be done with the covariant protocol
[
65
]
, satisfying
\displaystyle p(U^{\prime}\hat{U}_{i}U^{\prime\prime}|U^{\prime}U_{\mathrm{in}}U^{\prime\prime})=p(\hat{U}_{i}|U_{\mathrm{in}})\quad\forall U^{\prime},U^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{U}(d).
(101)
By converting the estimation-based protocol (
98
) using the covariant unitary estimation as shown in Eq. (
70
), we obtain an isometry adjointation protocol given by [see Fig.
5
(b)]
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{a}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}](\rho_{\mathrm{in}})\coloneqq\sum_{i}(\mathcal{R}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{i})\circ(\Psi_{a}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}})[\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\otimes(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}})(\phi)]
(102)
for all  a\in\{I,O\} ,  V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)  and  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{D}) , where  \Psi_{a}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime n}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{\prime})  is a quantum instrument implemented by Algorithm
1
.
Note that the quantum instrument  \{\Psi_{a}\}_{a}  is efficiently implementable using an efficient implementation of the quantum Schur transform
[
53
,
54
,
55
,
56
,
58
]
.
The bottleneck of Algorithm
1
is the quantum Schur transform  \mathrm{Sch}_{n+1,D} , which can be implemented with the precision  \epsilon^{\prime}  with the circuit complexity  O(\mathrm{poly}(n,D,\log\epsilon^{\prime-1}))
[
53
,
54
]
or  O(\mathrm{poly}(n,\log D,\log\epsilon^{\prime-1}))
[
55
]
7
7
7
Note that the result by Ref.
[
55
]
is challenged by several researchers
[
66
,
67
]
.
.
The approximation error  \epsilon  of isometry adjointation is given in the following Theorem.
Theorem 5
.
The parallel protocol shown in Fig.
5
(b) implements an isometry adjointation with the detection error  \alpha=\alpha_{\phi}  and the decode error  \eta=1-F_{\mathrm{est}}  in Eq. (
25
), where  \alpha_{\phi}  is defined by
\displaystyle\alpha_{\phi}
\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}\Tr[\Tr_{\mathcal{A}}(\phi)\Pi_{\lambda}^{(d)}]\sum_{\mu\in\lambda+_{d}\square}{\mathrm{hook}(\lambda)\over\mathrm{hook}(\mu)}
(103)
\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}\Tr[\Tr_{\mathcal{A}}(\phi)\Pi_{\lambda}^{(d)}]\left[1-\sum_{\mu\in\lambda+\square\setminus\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n+1}}{\mathrm{hook}(\lambda)\over\mathrm{hook}(\mu)}\right],
(104)
\mathrm{hook}(\mu)
is defined by Eq. (
58
), and  F_{\mathrm{est}}  is the entanglement fidelity of the covariant unitary estimation.
Thus, the worse-case diamond-norm error is given by
\displaystyle\epsilon
\displaystyle=\max\{\alpha_{\phi},1-F_{\mathrm{est}}\}.
(105)
Proof.
See Appendix
A.5
for the proof.
