3.3
Reduction to isometry inversion and universal error detection
By discarding the measurement outcome from the isometry adjointation protocols, we obtain deterministic isometry inversion protocols shown in Figs.
3
(c-1) and
5
(c-1), which are given by replacing  \Gamma_{a}^{(n+1)}  and  \Psi_{a}  in the original isometry adjointation protocols with  \Gamma^{(n+1)}\coloneqq\sum_{a}\Gamma_{a}^{(n+1)}  and  \Psi\coloneqq\sum_{a}\Psi_{a} , respectively.
The worst-case channel fidelity of the derived isometry inversion protocol is shown to be the same as the original unitary inversion protocol (see Appendix
A.6.1
).
By replacing the original unitary inversion protocol with the probabilistic exact one, we obtain the probabilistic exact isometry inversion protocols as shown in Figs.
3
(c-2),
5
(c-2).
The parallel protocol for probabilistic exact isometry inversion is the same as that shown in Ref.
[
36
]
.
The derived protocols achieve the same success probability as the untiary inversion protocol (see Appendix
A.6.2
).
In conclusion, we obtain the following Corollary.
Corollary 6
.
Suppose there exists a parallel or sequential protocol for probabilistic exact (deterministic)  d -dimensional unitary inversion achieving success probability  p_{\mathrm{UI}}  (average-case channel fidelity  F_{\mathrm{UI}} ) using  n  calls of  U_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{U}(d) . Then, we can construct a parallel protocol for probabilistic exact (deterministic) isometry inversion for  V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)  achieving success probability  p=p_{\mathrm{UI}}  (worst-case channel fidelity  F=F_{\mathrm{UI}} ) using  n  calls of  V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D) .
Reference
[
30
]
shows a deterministic exact sequential protocol for qubit-unitary inversion using four calls of the input qubit-unitary operation  U_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{U}(2) . Combining this protocol with Theorem
6
, we can construct a deterministic exact sequential protocol for qubit-encoding isometry inversion using four calls of the input qubit-encoding isometry operation  V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(2,D)  for any  D\geq 2 .
By discarding the output state from the isometry adjointation protocols, we obtain universal error detection protocols shown in Figs.
3
(d) and
5
(d).
The sequential protocol shown in Fig.
3
(d) is obtained by discarding  \Lambda^{\prime\prime(n+1)}  in the original isometry adjointation protocol and replacing  \Gamma_{a}^{(n+1)}  and  \Lambda^{\prime(n)}  with the POVM measurement  \mathcal{G}_{a}\coloneqq\Tr\circ\Gamma_{a}^{(n+1)}  and the quantum channel  \Lambda^{\prime\prime(n)}\coloneqq\Tr_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}\circ\Lambda^{\prime(n)} , respectively.
The parallel protocol shown in Fig.
5
(d) is obtained by discarding  \mathcal{R}_{i}  and  M_{i}  in the original isometry adjointation protocol, and replacing  \phi  and  \Psi_{a}  with the quantum state  \phi^{\prime}\coloneqq\Tr_{\mathcal{A}}(\phi)  and the POVM measurement  \mathcal{P}_{a}\coloneqq\Tr\circ\Psi_{a} , respectively.
The approximation errors of the derived protocols are shown in the following Corollary (see Appendix
A.6.3
).
Corollary 7
.
The sequential and parallel protocols shown in Figs.
3
(d) and
5
(d) implement a universal error detection with the approximation errors  \alpha^{(x)}  for  x=\mathrm{SEQ}  (sequential protocol) and  x=\mathrm{PAR}  (parallel protocol) given by
\displaystyle\alpha^{(\mathrm{SEQ})}
\displaystyle=\Tr(C''\Sigma'),
(109)
\displaystyle\alpha^{(\mathrm{PAR})}
\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}\Tr(\phi' \Pi_\lambda^{(d)})\sum_{\mu\in\lambda+_{d}\square}{\mathrm{hook}(\lambda)\over\mathrm{hook}(\mu)}
(110)
\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}\Tr(\phi' \Pi_\lambda^{(d)})\left[1-\sum_{\mu\in\lambda+\square\setminus\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n+1}}{\mathrm{hook}(\lambda)\over\mathrm{hook}(\mu)}\right],
(111)
where  C^{\prime\prime}  is the Choi operator of the quantum comb given by  C^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq J_{\Lambda^{\prime(1)}}\star J_{\Lambda^{\prime(2)}}\star\cdots\star J_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime(n)}} ,  \Sigma^{\prime}  is defined by  \Sigma^{\prime}\coloneqq\Tr_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{n}}\Sigma  using  \Sigma  defined in Eq. (
92
), and  \phi^{\prime}  is a quantum state shown in the protocol
5
(d).
3.4
Relationship to programmable projective measurement
Reference
[
4
]
considers a task to construct a projective measurement  \{\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi},\mathds{1}-\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\}  from  n  copies of an unknown quantum state  \ket{\psi}\in\mathbb{C}^{D} . The task is to construct a measurement  \{\Pi_{I},\Pi_{O}\}  such that
\displaystyle\Tr(\Pi_I \ketbra{\psi}{\psi})
\displaystyle=1,
(112)
\displaystyle\Tr(\Pi_I \ketbra{\psi^{\perp}}{\psi^{\perp}})
\displaystyle=\alpha\;\;\;(\forall\ket{\psi^{\perp}}\perp\ket{\psi}).
(113)
This task can be considered as a special case ( d=1 ) of universal error detection. Reference
[
4
]
shows that the optimal failure probability is given by
\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{1}{n+1}.
(114)
The optimal success probability is achieved by a protocol shown in Fig.
6
, where  \mathcal{M}=\{M_{I},M_{O}\}  is a POVM defined by  M_{I}=\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}  and  M_{O}=\mathds{1}-\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}} , where  \Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}  is an orthonormal projector onto the totally symmetric subspace of  (\mathbb{C}^{D})^{\otimes n+1} . This protocol corresponds to the universal error detection protocol for  d=1 .
The universal error detection protocol is a generalization of programmable projective measurement for rank- d  (destructive) projective measurement given by  \{\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathds{1}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}\}  using an isometry operator  V_{\mathrm{in}}:\mathbb{C}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}^{D} . In particular, the parallel protocol shown in Fig.
5
(d) can be regarded as an implementation of the rank- d  projective measurement using a program state  \mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}(\phi^{\prime})  (see also Section
5.2
).
Figure 6:
Optimal protocol for programmable projective measurement shown in Ref.
[
4
]
.
4
Analysis of the optimal protocols
In Section
4.1
, we reintroduce the Choi operator of the quantum supermap to describe the general superinstrument that is not implementable by the quantum circuit.
We show that the optimal protocols for isometry adjointation, isometry inversion, and universal error detection can be found in the Choi operators satisfying the unitary group symmetry.
We utilize the unitary group symmetry to investigate the optimal performances analytically in Section
4.2
and numerically in Section
4.3
.
4.1
Choi representation of general superinstruments and  \mathbb{U}(d)\times\mathbb{U}(D)  symmetry of the tasks
Similarly to Eq. (
38
) for a sequential protocol, a general superchannel  \mathcal{C}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}[\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{i})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{i})]\to[\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})]  can be represented in the Choi operator  C  satisfying
\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{C}[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}]}=C\star\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}J_{\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(i)}},
(115)
where  J_{\Phi}  is the Choi operator of a quantum channel  \Phi , and  C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{F})  is the Choi operator of  \mathcal{C} . The set of superchannels implemented by parallel ( x=\mathrm{PAR} ) and sequential ( x=\mathrm{SEQ} ) protocols, and the set of general superchannel ( x=\mathrm{GEN} ) can be characterized by the positivity and linear conditions on  C  as
\displaystyle C
\displaystyle\geq 0,
(116)
\displaystyle C
\displaystyle\in\mathcal{W}^{(x)},
(117)
where  \mathcal{W}^{(x)}  is a linear subspace of  \mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{F})  [see Appendix
E.1
for the definition of  \mathcal{W}^{(x)} ]. The case  x=\mathrm{SEQ}  corresponds to Theorem
1
.
A general superinstrument  \mathcal{C}_{a}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}[\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}_{i})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}_{i})]\to[\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F})]  can also be represented in the Choi operator  C_{a}  satisfying
\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{C}_{a}[\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n)}]}=C_{a}\star\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}J_{\Phi_{\mathrm{in}}^{(i)}}.
(118)
The set of superinstruments implemented by parallel ( x=\mathrm{PAR} ) and sequential ( x=\mathrm{SEQ} ) protocols, and the set of general superinstrument ( x=\mathrm{GEN} ) can be characterized by the positivity and linear conditions on  C\coloneqq\sum_{a}C_{a}  as
\displaystyle C_{a}
\displaystyle\geq 0,
(119)
\displaystyle C\coloneqq\sum_{a}C_{a}
\displaystyle\in\mathcal{W}^{(x)}.
(120)
The case  x=\mathrm{SEQ}  corresponds to Theorem
2
.
The protocols for isometry inversion, universal error detection, and isometry adjointation can be represented by the Choi operators of the corresponding superchannel or superinstrument given by
\displaystyle\begin{cases}C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{F})&(\mathrm{deterministic\;isometry\;inversion})\\
\{C_{S},C_{F}\}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{F})&(\mathrm{probabilistic\;exact\;isometry\;inversion})\\
\{C_{I},C_{O}\}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{P})&(\mathrm{universal\;error\;detection})\\
\{C_{I},C_{O}\}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{I}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{F})&(\mathrm{isometry\;adjointation})\end{cases}.
(121)
The optimization of the Choi operators can be done under the  \mathbb{U}(d)\times\mathbb{U}(D)  symmetry as shown in the following Theorem.
Theorem 8
.
The optimal performances of isometry inversion, universal error detection, and isometry adjointation can be searched within Choi operators satisfying
\displaystyle\begin{cases}[C,U^{\prime\otimes n+1}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathcal{F}}\otimes U^{\prime\prime\otimes n+1}_{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{O}^{n}}]=0&(\mathrm{deterministic\;isometry\;inversion})\\
[C_{a},U^{\prime\otimes n+1}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathcal{F}}\otimes U^{\prime\prime\otimes n+1}_{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{O}^{n}}]=0\quad\forall a\in\{S,F\}&(\mathrm{probabilistic\;exact\;isometry\;inversion})\\
[C_{a},U^{\prime\otimes n}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}}\otimes U^{\prime\prime\otimes n+1}_{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{O}^{n}}]=0\quad\forall a\in\{I,O\}&(\mathrm{universal\;error\;detection})\\
[C_{a},U^{\prime\otimes n+1}_{\mathcal{I}^{n}\mathcal{F}}\otimes U^{\prime\prime\otimes n+1}_{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{O}^{n}}]=0\quad\forall a\in\{I,O\}&(\mathrm{isometry\;adjointation})\end{cases}
(122)
for all  U^{\prime}\in\mathbb{U}(d)  and  U^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{U}(D) .
Proof.
See Appendix
B.1
for the proof.
∎
The universal error detection having the  \mathbb{U}(d)\times\mathbb{U}(D)  symmetry is shown to output the POVM in the form of Eq. (
20
).
Theorem 9
.
The universal error detection protocol with the Choi operator satisfying the  \mathbb{U}(d)\times\mathbb{U}(D)  symmetry shown in Theorem
8
outputs the POVMs given by Eq. (
20
).
Similarly, the isometry adjointation protocol with the Choi operator satisfying the  \mathbb{U}(d)\times\mathbb{U}(D)  symmetry shown in Theorem
8
outputs the quantum instrument given by Eq. (
25
).
Proof.
As shown in Lemma
17
in Appendix
E.2
, if the Choi operator of the universal error detection protocol satisfies the  \mathbb{U}(d)\times\mathbb{U}(D)  symmetry (
122
), its output POVM  \{\Pi_{a}\}  is given by
\displaystyle\Pi_{a}=v_{a}\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}+w_{a}(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}})\quad\forall a\in\{I,O\},V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)
(123)
using  v_{I},v_{O},w_{I},w_{O}\in\mathbb{C} .
From the one-sided error condition (
15
), the output POVM is given by Eq. (
20
).
Similarly, if the Choi operator of the isometry adjointation protocol satisfies the  \mathbb{U}(d)\times\mathbb{U}(D)  symmetry (
122
), its output instrument is given by
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{a}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n})(\rho_{\mathrm{in}})=x_{a}V_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}\rho_{\mathrm{in}}V_{\mathrm{in}}+{\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{F}}\over d}\Tr[\rho_{\mathrm{in}}(y_{a}\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}+z_{a}(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}}))]
\displaystyle\forall a\in\{I,O\},V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)
(124)
using  x_{I},x_{O},y_{I},y_{O},z_{I},z_{O}\in\mathbb{C} .
From the one-sided error condition (
21
), the output instrument is given by Eq. (
25
).
∎
4.2
Optimal construction of isometry inversion, universal error detection, and isometry adjointation protocols
We show that the construction of parallel or sequential protocols of isometry adjointation, isometry inversion, and universal error detection are the optimal, as shown in the following Theorem.
Theorem 10
.
The parallel or sequential protocols for probabilistic exact isometry inversion, deterministic isometry inversion, universal error detection, and isometry adjointation shown in Theorems
4
and
5
and Corollaries
6
and
7
achieve the optimal performances among all parallel or sequential protocols, respectively.
Proof.
See Appendix
C
for the proof.
∎
Since the figure of merits shown in Theorems
4
and
5
and Corollaries
6
and
7
do not depend on  D , we can show that the optimal performances do not depend on  D .
Corollary 11
.
For  D>d  and  x\in\{\mathrm{PAR},\mathrm{SEQ}\} , the following relations hold:
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)
\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,d+1,n),
(125)
\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)
\displaystyle=F_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,d,n),
(126)
\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)
\displaystyle=p_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,d,n),
(127)
\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)
\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,d+1,n).
(128)
Using Theorem
10
, we analyze the optimal protocols for isometry inversion, universal error detection, and isometry adjointation in the following subsections.
4.2.1
Isometry inversion
The optimal success probability and fidelity of isometry inversion are given by those of unitary inversion when we use parallel or sequential protocols, as shown in Corollary
11
. It is already shown in Ref.
[
36
]
for the parallel protocol, but the sequential protocol case is newly shown in this work, which is conjectured in Ref.
[
36
]
.
To investigate the generalization of Corollary
11
for general protocols including indefinite causal order, we calculate the optimal probability or worst-case channel fidelity numerically (see Section
4.3
for the detail).
Numerical results show that a similar equation does not hold for general protocols including indefinite causal order, i.e.,  p_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{GEN})}(d,D,n)
7
).
This behavior is compatible with the fact that the composition of a general quantum supermap with a quantum comb does not yield a valid quantum supermap in general
[
70
]
, so the construction of isometry inversion protocols shown in Fig.
3
(c) cannot be applied for general protocols.
Figure 7:
(a) Optimal success probability  p_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{GEN})}  and (b) optimal worst-case channel fidelity  F_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{GEN})}  of isometry inversion among general protocols including indefinite causal order. Solid lines represent the case  D=d  (unitary inversion), and dashed lines represent the case  D=d+1  for  d=2  (
\bullet
),  d=3  (
\blacktriangle
), and  d=4  (
\blacksquare
). The optimal values shown here are obtained by numerical calculations, and numerical values are shown in Appendix
D
.
4.2.2
Universal error detection
The optimal performance of parallel protocol  \alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{PAR})}(d,D,n)  is given as follows.
Theorem 12
.
\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{PAR})}(d,D,n)={1\over d+k+1}\left(d+{d-l\over d+k-l}\right)={d^{2}\over n}+O(d^{2}n^{-2}),
(129)
where  k  and  l  are given by  n=kd+l  ( k\in\mathbb{Z},0\leq l
Proof.
See Appendix
B.2
for the proof.
∎
From Theorem
12
and the result in Ref.
[
4
]
, we can show the following property on the scaling of  \alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)  with respect to  n .
Corollary 13
.
\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)=\Theta(n^{-1})\quad\forall D>d,\forall x\in\{\mathrm{PAR},\mathrm{SEQ},\mathrm{GEN}\}
(130)
holds for an arbitrary fixed value of  d .
Proof.
From Theorem
12
, we obtain
\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{PAR})}(d,D,n)=O(n^{-1}).
(131)
The Hilbert space  \mathbb{C}^{D}  can be embedded onto  \mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)  by identifying  \ket{\psi}\in\mathbb{C}^{D}  with  V=\outerproduct{\psi}{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\outerproduct{\psi_i}{i}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)  for a set of orthonormal vectors  \{\ket{\psi_i}\}_{i=1}^{d-1}  satisfying  \innerproduct{\psi_i}{\psi}=0  for all  i\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\} . Therefore, a universal error detection protocol for  V\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)  can simulate a programmable projective measurement for  \ket{\psi}\in\mathbb{C}^{D} , which leads to
\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)\geq{1\over n+1}=\Omega(n^{-1}).
(132)
Thus, we obtain  \alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(x)}(d,D,n)=\Theta(n^{-1}). 
∎
4.2.3
Isometry adjointation
The optimal scaling of the approximation error of parallel isometry adjointation is given as follows.
Theorem 14
.
\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{PAR})}(d,D,n)=\Theta(d^{2}n^{-1}).
(133)
Proof.
First,  \epsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{PAR})}(d,D,n)\geq\alpha_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{PAR})}  holds since any isometry adjointation protocol can be converted to an error detection protocol by discarding the output state of an isometry adjointation protocol. Thus, we obtain  \epsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{PAR})}(d,D,n)=\Omega(d^{2}n^{-1})  from Theorem
12
. On the other hand, from the construction shown in Section
3.2.2
, we see that  \epsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{PAR})}(d,D,n)=O(d^{2}n^{-1})  (see Appendix
B.3
). Thus, we obtain  \epsilon_{\mathrm{opt}}^{(\mathrm{PAR})}(d,D,n)=\Theta(d^{2}n^{-1}) .
∎
4.3
Numerical results for the optimal protocols
From numerical calculations, we investigate the optimal protocols for isometry inversion, universal error detection, and isometry adjointation. We represent the protocols implementing isometry inversion, universal error detection, and isometry adjointation by their Choi operators and formulate the optimization of the figure of merits within the possible protocols as SDP. We utilize a  \mathbb{U}(d)\times\mathbb{U}(D)  symmetry of the Choi operator (Theorem
8
) to simplify the SDP, which is a similar technique presented in Ref.
[
30
]
(see also Ref.
[
71
]
).
See Appendix
E
for the details of the derivation of the SDP.
We calculate the derived SDP in
MATLAB
[
72
]
using the interpreter
CVX
[
73
,
74
]
with the solvers
SDPT3
[
75
,
76
,
77
]
,
SeDuMi
[
78
]
and
MOSEK
[
79
]
. Group-theoretic calculations to write down the SDP are done with
SageMath
[
80
]
.
See Appendix
D
for the numerical results.
All codes are available at Ref.
[
81
]
under the MIT license
[
82
]
.
The optimal performances for parallel or sequential protocols are calculated for the case of  D=d+1  since they do not depend on  D  (see Corollary
11
), which is shown by the construction of protocols shown in Figs.
3
and
5
.
Although the same construction is impossible for general protocols including indefinite causal order, we also see that the optimal performances for general protocols do not depend on  D  as long as  D\geq d+1  holds by checking the values for  D=d+1,\ldots,d+10 .
However, the maximum success probability (channel fidelity) of probabilistic exact (deterministic) isometry inversion for the case of  D=d  (unitary inversion) is different from those for the case of  D=d+1  (isometry inversion), as shown in Tables
5
and
6
(corresponding to Fig.
7
in Section
4.2.1
).
We also see the advantage of indefinite causal order over sequential protocols in isometry inversion and universal error detection, but the advantage disappears in the case of isometry adjointation.
5
Discussions on the potential applications
Finally, we discuss the potential applications of this work.
5.1
Petz recovery map
Petz recovery map is an almost optimal decoding channel from a noise channel and is widely used in approximate quantum error correction
[
83
,
84
]
, quantum communication
[
85
]
, and information scrambling
[
86
,
87
,
88
]
.
For a quantum channel  \mathcal{N}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B})  and a quantum state  \sigma\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}) , the Petz recovery map  \mathcal{P}^{\sigma,\mathcal{N}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})  is defined by
[
89
]
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\sigma,\mathcal{N}}(\cdot)\coloneqq\sigma^{1/2}\mathcal{N}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{N}(\sigma)^{-1/2}\cdot\mathcal{N}(\sigma)^{-1/2})\sigma^{1/2}.
(134)
An implementation of the Petz recovery map is shown in Ref.
[
90
]
, which requires
•
The quantum circuit of the block-encoding unitaries  U^{\sigma}  and  U^{\mathcal{N}(\sigma)}  of  \sigma  and  \mathcal{N}(\sigma) , respectively.
•
The quantum circuit  U^{\mathcal{N}}:\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}  such that  U^{\mathcal{N}}\ket{0}_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}=V^{\mathcal{N}}  is a Stinespring dilation of  \mathcal{N} , i.e.,  \Tr_{\mathcal{E}}[V^{\mathcal{N}}\cdot V^{\mathcal{N}\dagger}]=\mathcal{N}(\cdot) .
The implementation shown in Ref.
[
90
]
composes the following three maps:
\displaystyle(\cdot)
\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{N}(\sigma)^{-1/2}(\cdot)\mathcal{N}(\sigma)^{-1/2},
(135)
\displaystyle(\cdot)
\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{N}^{\dagger}(\cdot),
(136)
\displaystyle(\cdot)
\displaystyle\mapsto\sigma^{1/2}(\cdot)\sigma^{1/2}.
(137)
The maps (
135
) and (
137
) are implemented by using the quantum singular value transformation applied on  U^{\sigma}  and  U^{\mathcal{N}(\sigma)} .
The map (
136
) is implemented by using the following equation:
\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\dagger}(\cdot)\propto\Tr_{\overline{\mathcal{E}}}\left[\bra{0}_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}U^{\mathcal{N}\dagger}\left(\outerproduct{\phi^+}{\phi^+}_{\mathcal{E}\overline{\mathcal{E}}}\otimes\cdot\right)U^{\mathcal{N}}\ket{0}_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\right],
(138)
or equivalently,
\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\dagger}(\cdot)\propto\Tr_{\overline{\mathcal{E}}}\left[V^{\mathcal{N}\dagger}\left(\outerproduct{\phi^+}{\phi^+}_{\mathcal{E}\overline{\mathcal{E}}}\otimes\cdot\right)V^{\mathcal{N}}\right].
(139)
Thus, using the inverse of  U^{\mathcal{N}} , one can implement the map (
136
).
However, it is a too strong assumption that the quantum circuit of  U^{\mathcal{N}}  (or the inverse of  U^{\mathcal{N}} ) is given in several situations.
For instance, the Petz recovery map can be used for the information recovery from an information scrambling dynamics followed by an erasure error
[
86
,
87
,
88
]
, and  U^{\mathcal{N}}  corresponds to the information scrambling dynamics in this case.
To implement the inverse of  U^{\mathcal{N}}  in this case, it is required to implement the backward time evolution, which is only possible in few-body systems in the current experimental settings
[
91
,
92
,
93
,
94
]
.
One can also utilize  D -dimensional unitary inversion for  D=\dim(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}) , but it requires  \Theta(D^{2})  calls of  U^{\mathcal{N}}
[
30
,
34
,
95
]
.
If we use isometry adjointation instead, one can implement  V^{\mathcal{N}\dagger}  with approximation error  \epsilon  by using  O(d^{2}/\epsilon)  calls of  V^{\mathcal{N}}  for  d=\dim\mathcal{A}  (see Theorem
5
).
Therefore, for  D\gg d , our protocol for isometry adjointation works better than unitary inversion.
5.2
Universal programming of two-outcome projective measurement
A universal programmable quantum processor implements quantum operations specified by a quantum state called the program state
[
96
]
.
In general, for a set of quantum operations  \mathbb{S}\coloneqq\{\Lambda\}_{\Lambda} , we define the task to implement a set of the program states  \{\phi_{\Lambda}\}_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{S}}  and the quantum channel  \mathcal{D}  such that
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\otimes\phi_{\Lambda})=\Lambda(\rho_{\mathrm{in}})
(140)
for any input state  \rho_{\mathrm{in}}  and any  \Lambda\in\mathbb{S} .
For the set of probabilistic operations  \mathbb{S}=\{\mathcal{M}=\{\Lambda_{a}\}\}_{\mathcal{M}} , we replace  \mathcal{D}  with the probabilistic operation  \{\mathcal{D}_{a}\}  and the task is to implement
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{a}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\otimes\phi_{\mathcal{M}})=\Lambda_{a}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}}).
(141)
It is shown that an exact universal programmable quantum processor is impossible for unitary operations
[
96
]
and for projective measurements
[
97
,
98
]
, and approximate or probabilistic implementations are investigated (e.g., Refs.
[
96
,
97
,
98
,
99
,
100
,
101
]
for projective or POVM measurements).
In the approximate implementation, the tradeoff between the size of the program state called the program cost, and the approximation error is investigated (e.g., Refs.
[
100
,
102
]
for POVM measurements).
The program cost is defined by
\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{S}}\coloneqq\log\left[\dim\bigoplus_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{Supp}(\phi_{\Lambda})\right],
(142)
where  \mathrm{Supp}(\phi_{\Lambda})  is the support of  \phi_{\Lambda}  defined by  \mathrm{Supp}(\phi_{\Lambda})\coloneqq\mathrm{span}\{\ket{\psi}\mid\phi_{\Lambda}\ket{\psi}\neq 0\} .
For the universal programming of POVM measurements, we consider the worst-case operational distance as the approximation error defined by
[
100
,
102
]
\displaystyle\delta\coloneqq\inf_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{S}}D_{\mathrm{op}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}),
(143)
where  \mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\{P^{\prime}_{a}\}  is the POVM defined by  \Tr[P^{\prime}_{a}\cdot]\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{a}(\cdot\otimes\phi_{\mathcal{M}})  and  D_{\mathrm{op}}  is the operational distance defined in Eq. (
17
).
The parallel universal error detection protocol shown in Fig.
5
(d) can implement universal programming of rank- d  projective measurement, where  \mathbb{S}  is given by
\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{PVM}}^{(d,D)}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{\Pi,\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi\}\mid\Pi\text{ is an orthogonal projector with rank }d\},
(144)
by using the program state  \phi_{\mathcal{M}}=\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}(\phi^{\prime})  for  V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)  such that  \Pi=\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}} .
Since any two-outcome POVM measurement can be realized as a probabilistic mixture of two-outcome projective measurements
[
103
,
104
]
, we can similarly implement universal programming of two-outcome POVM measurement, where  \mathbb{S}  is given by
\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{POVM}}^{(D)}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{\Pi,\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi\}\mid 0\leq\Pi\leq\mathds{1}_{D}\}.
(145)
We show the following Corollary from Theorem
12
:
Corollary 15
.
There exists a protocol for universal programming of rank- d  projective measurement with the approximation error  \delta  and the program cost given by
\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{PVM}}^{(d,D)}}\leq d(D-d)\log{\Theta(Dd)\over\delta}.
(146)
Similarly, there exists a protocol for universal programming of two-outcome POVM measurement with the approximation error  \delta  and the program cost given by
\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{POVM}}^{(D)}}\leq\begin{cases}{D^{2}\over 4}\log{\Theta(D^2)\over\delta}&(D\mathrm{\;is\;even})\\
{D^{2}-1\over 4}\log{\Theta(D^2)\over\delta}&(D\mathrm{\;is\;odd})\\
\end{cases}.
(147)
Proof.
See Appendix
F
for the proof.
∎
Note that Ref.
[
100
]
proposes universal programming of  D -dimensional  D -outcome projective measurements, where  \mathbb{S}  is given by
\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{PVM}}^{(D)}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{\outerproduct{\psi_1}{\psi_1},\ldots,\outerproduct{\psi_D}{\psi_D}\}\mid\innerproduct{\psi_i}{\psi_j}=\delta_{ij}\quad\forall i,j\},
(148)
with the program cost  C_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{PVM}}^{(D)}}\leq D(D-1)\log{\Theta(D^{5\over 2})\over\delta} .
We obtain a tighter bound of the program cost in Eq. (
146
) by restricting the measurement to two-outcome projective measurements.
We conjecture the program cost (
146
) to be optimal from the following argument based on the parameter counting.
In Ref.
[
14
]
, the optimal program cost of unitary operations with  \nu  real parameters is conjecture to be
\displaystyle C={\nu\over 2}\log{C_{\nu, d}\over\epsilon},
(149)
where  \epsilon  is the diamond-norm approximation error, and  C_{\nu,d}  is a parameter independent of  \epsilon .
By assuming this conjecture holds for the projective measurements by substituting  \epsilon  with  \delta , we can show that the program cost (
146
) is optimal.
The rank- d  projective measurement can be uniquely specified by a  d -dimensional subspace of  \mathbb{C}^{d} , which can be specified by  2d(D-d)  real parameters
8
8
8
The  d -dimensional subspace of  \mathbb{C}^{d}  can be represented as  \mathrm{span}\{\ket{\psi_1},\ldots,\ket{\psi_d}\}  by using an orthonormal basis  \{\ket{\psi_1},\ldots,\ket{\psi_d}\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{D} , i.e., the set satisfying  \innerproduct{\psi_i}{\psi_j}=1  for all  i,j .
This representation is unique up to the following action of  \mathbb{U}(d) :  \ket{\psi_i}\mapsto\sum_{j}u_{ij}\ket{\psi_j}  for  (u_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{d}\in\mathbb{U}(d) .
The number of real parameters to specify an orthonormal basis  \{\ket{\psi_1},\ldots,\ket{\psi_d}\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{D}  is given by  2Dd-d^{2} , and the number of real parameters to specify an element of  \mathbb{U}(d)  is given by  d^{2} .
Therefore, the number of real parameters to specify a  d -dimensional subspace of  \mathbb{C}^{d}  can be given as  2Dd-d^{2}-d^{2}=2d(D-d) .
.
Therefore, by assuming the conjecture, we can show that the optimal program cost of rank- d  projective measurement is given by
\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{PVM}}^{(d,D)}}=d(D-d)\log{C_{d,D} \over\delta},
(150)
where  C_{d,D}  is a parameter independent of  \delta .
6
Conclusion
In this work, we investigate the universal transformation of isometry operations to explore the possible transformation in higher-order quantum computation. We define the task called isometry adjointation, which is to transform the isometry operation into its adjoint operation.
The isometry adjointation protocol is constructed by converting the unitary inversion protocol using the probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{I},\mathcal{T}_{O}\} .
Using the idea of composition of quantum combs, the problem of designing isometry adjointation protocol reduces to designing a unitary inversion protocol, which is extensively studied in previous works
[
10
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
25
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
]
.
In special cases, the isometry adjointation reduces to unitary inversion (transformation of unitary operations) and programmable projective measurement (transformation of pure states).
Due to such reducibility, isometry adjointation is a useful task to understand the difference between higher-order quantum computation and “lower-order” quantum computation (i.e., transformation of states), which are exhibited in several examples such as distinguishability
[
105
]
and superreplication
[
106
,
17
,
18
]
, under a unified formulation.
We also construct isometry inversion and universal error detection protocols by discarding measurement outcome and output quantum state, respectively.
We show that our construction gives the optimal performances among all parallel or sequential protocols, which implies that the optimal performances among parallel or sequential protocols do not depend on the output dimension  D  of the isometry operation.
We analyze the optimal performances of isometry adjointation and show that the optimal approximation error  \epsilon  of the parallel isometry adjointation protocol is given by  \epsilon=\Theta(d^{2}n^{-1}) , where  d  is the input dimension of the isometry operation, and  n  is the number of calls of the input operation.
To investigate the general protocols including indefinite causal order, we also provide the numerical results for the optimal performances of isometry adjointation, isometry inversion, and universal error detection using the semidefinite programming combined with the unitary group symmetry.
The optimal performances of probabilistic exact (deterministic) unitary inversion ( D=d ) are different from those of isometry inversion ( D\geq d+1 ), which is compatible with the impossibility of the composition of a general supermap with a quantum comb.
However, numerical results also show that the optimal performances of isometry adjointation and universal error detection using the general protocol do not depend on  D .
We also see the advantage of indefinite causal order protocols over sequential protocols in isometry inversion and universal error detection, but the advantage disappears in isometry adjointation.
We show the potential application of this work for the implementation of the Petz recovery map and universal programming of two-outcome projective measurement.
This work identifies that unitary inversion protocol can be converted to isometry adjointation with  D -independent approximation error.
However, Ref.
[
36
]
shows that isometry complex conjugation is impossible while unitary complex conjugation is possible
[
20
]
, and conjectures that the optimal success probability of isometry transposition depends on  D .
From this observation, it is not trivial whether a given universal transformation of unitary operations can be converted to the corresponding transformation of isometry operations.
We leave it an open problem to investigate whether such a conversion is possible for other universal transformations of unitary operations.

Appendix A
Construction of isometry adjointation protocols
A.1
Evaluation of the diamond-norm error of isometry adjointation
We evaluate the diamond-norm error of isometry adjointation protocol satisfying Eq. (
25
).
First,  \mathcal{C}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}]-\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{adjoint}}  decomposes into two completely positive maps  \Phi_{1}  and  \Phi_{2}  as
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}]-\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{adjoint}}
\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2},
(151)
\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}})
\displaystyle\coloneqq-{d^{2}\eta\over d^{2}-1}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\dagger}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}})-\Tr(\Pi_{\Im V_\mathrm{in}}\rho_\mathrm{in})]\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0},
(152)
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(\rho_{\mathrm{in}})
\displaystyle\coloneqq\alpha\Tr[(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}})\rho_{\mathrm{in}}]\left({\mathds{1}_{d}\over d}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}-{\mathds{1}_{d}\over d}\otimes\outerproduct{1}{1}\right),
(153)
whose kernels do not intersect, i.e.,  \ker\Phi_{1}\cap\ker\Phi_{2}=\emptyset . Since the diamond norm  \|\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2}\|_{\diamond}  is defined by
\displaystyle\|\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2}\|_{\diamond}\coloneqq\sup_{\psi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{A}),\|\psi\|_{1}\leq 1}\|(\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2})\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(\psi)\|_{1}
(154)
using an auxiliary Hilbert space  \mathcal{A}  and the trace norm  \|\cdot\|_{1} , it is given by
\displaystyle\|\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2}\|_{\diamond}
\displaystyle=\sup_{\psi_{i}\in\ker\Phi_{i}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}),\|\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}\|_{1}\leq 1}\|(\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2})\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(\psi_{1}+\psi_{2})\|_{1}
(155)
\displaystyle=\max_{0\leq x\leq 1}[x\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi_{1}\in\ker\Phi_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\\
\|\psi_{1}\|_{1}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\|\Phi_{1}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(\psi_{1})\|_{1}+(1-x)\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi_{2}\in\ker\Phi_{2}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\\
\|\psi_{2}\|_{1}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\|\Phi_{1}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(\psi_{2})\|_{1}]
(156)
\displaystyle=\max\{\|\Phi_{1}\|_{\diamond},\|\Phi_{2}\|_{\diamond}\}.
(157)
The diamond norm  \|\Phi_{1}\|_{\diamond}  is given by
\displaystyle\|\Phi_{1}\|_{\diamond}
\displaystyle=\|\Phi_{1}\circ\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}\|_{\diamond}
(158)
\displaystyle=\|\mathds{1}_{d}-\mathcal{D}_{q}\|_{\diamond}
(159)
\displaystyle=2\eta,
(160)
where  \mathcal{D}_{q}  is the depolarizing channel
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{q}(\rho)\coloneqq(1-q)\rho+q{\mathds{1}_{d}\over d}\Tr(\rho),
(161)
q  is given by  q\coloneqq{d^{2}\over d^{2}-1}\eta , and we utilize the fact that
[
107
]
\displaystyle\|\mathds{1}_{d}-\mathcal{D}_{q}\|_{\diamond}={2(d^{2}-1)\over d^{2}}q.
(162)
The diamond norm  \|\Phi_{2}\|_{\diamond}  is given by
\displaystyle\|\Phi_{2}\|_{\diamond}
\displaystyle=\sup_{\psi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{A}),\|\psi\|_{1}\leq 1}\|\alpha\Tr_{\mathcal{P}}[(\mathds{1}_{D}-\Pi_{{\mathrm{Im}}V_{\mathrm{in}}})\psi]\otimes({\mathds{1}_{d}\over d}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}-{\mathds{1}_{d}\over d}\otimes\outerproduct{1}{1})\|_{1}
(163)
\displaystyle=2\alpha.
(164)
Thus, we obtain
\displaystyle\epsilon
\displaystyle\coloneqq{1\over 2}\sup_{V_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{iso}}(d,D)}\|\mathcal{C}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\otimes n}]-\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{adjoint}}\|_{\diamond}
(165)
\displaystyle=\max\{\alpha,\eta\}.
(166)
A.2
Clebsch-Gordan transform and dual Clebsch-Gordan transform
In the next subsection, we construct the probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{I},\mathcal{T}_{O}\}  used for the construction of isometry adjointation protocols.
To this end, we first review the construction of the quantum Schur transform based on the concatenation of Clebsch-Gordan (CG) transforms.
Then, we show the dual CG transform, which was introduced to construct the mixed Schur transform.
Finally, we show a lemma on the dual CG transform, which will be used for the construction of the probabilistic quantum comb  \{\mathcal{T}_{I},\mathcal{T}_{O}\}  in the next subsection.
The quantum Schur transform can be implemented by using a series of CG transforms
[
53
,
54
]
.
For the irreducible representation  \mathcal{U}_{\lambda}^{(d)}  for  \lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n-1} , the tensor product space  \mathcal{U}_{\lambda}^{(d)}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}  can be decomposed as follows:
\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}^{(d)}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\lambda+_{d}\square}\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)},
(167)
where the vector  \ket{\lambda,u}_{\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}^{(d)}}\otimes\ket{j}_{\mathbb{C}^{d}}\in\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}^{(d)}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}  is given by
\displaystyle\ket{\lambda,u}_{\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}^{(d)}}\otimes\ket{j}_{\mathbb{C}^{d}}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\lambda+_{d}\square}\sum_{u^{\prime}}C^{\lambda,\mu}_{u,j,u^{\prime}}\ket{\mu,u'}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mu}^{(d)}}
(168)
using the coefficients  C^{\lambda,\mu}_{u,j,u^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}  called the CG coefficients.
The CG transform  \mathrm{CG}^{+}_{n,d}:\mathcal{T}_{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{n-1}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}\to\mathcal{T}_{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{n}  is defined by
\displaystyle\mathrm{CG}^{+}_{n,d}(\ket{\lambda}_{\mathcal{T}_{n-1}}\otimes\ket{u}_{\mathcal{U}_{n-1}}\otimes\ket{j}_{\mathbb{C}^{d}})\coloneqq\sum_{\mu\in\lambda+_{d}\square}\sum_{u^{\prime}}C^{\lambda,\mu}_{u,j,u^{\prime}}\ket{\lambda}_{\mathcal{T}_{n-1}}\otimes\ket{\mu}_{\mathcal{T}_{n}}\otimes\ket{u'}_{\mathcal{U}_{n}},
(169)
which can be represented as a controlled isometry as
\displaystyle\mathrm{CG}^{+}_{n,d}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n-1}}\outerproduct{\lambda}{\lambda}_{\mathcal{T}_{n-1}}\otimes\mathrm{CG}^{+}_{\lambda,d},
(170)
using  \mathrm{CG}^{+}_{\lambda,d}:\mathcal{U}_{n-1}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}\to\mathcal{T}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{n} .
By using the CG transform, we can construct the quantum Schur transform as follows (see Fig.
8
):
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sch}_{n,d}(\ket{j_1}_{\mathbb{C}^{d}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{j_n}_{\mathbb{C}^{d}})
\displaystyle=(\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{n-1}}\otimes\mathrm{CG}^{+}_{n,d})[\cdots[(\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}}\otimes\mathrm{CG}^{+}_{3,d})[\mathrm{CG}^{+}_{2,d}(\ket{\square}\otimes\ket{j_1}\otimes\ket{j_2})\otimes\ket{j_3}]\cdots]\otimes\ket{j_n}]
(171)
\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}^{d}_{n}}\sum_{u,i}C^{\mu,u,i}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}\ket{\mu_i^{(1)}\cdots\mu_i^{(n)}}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{T}_{n}}\otimes\ket{u}_{\mathcal{U}_{n}},
(172)
using coefficients  C^{\mu,u,i}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}\in\mathbb{C} ,
where the sequence of Young diagrams  \mu_{i}^{(1)}\cdots\mu_{i}^{(n)}  satisfying  \mu_{i}^{(j+1)}\in\mu_{i}^{(j)}+_{d}\square  for  j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}  is related to the standard Young tableau  s_{i}^{\mu}  such that  \mu_{i}^{(j)}  is the Young diagram obtained by removing the boxes  j+1 , …,  n  from the Young diagram  \mu  and  \mu_{i}^{(n)}=\mu .
For instance, the standard tableau

\@@amsalign
 holds, where we define $\ket{\phi^+_{\lambda, a}}\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes m}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{m}^{(d)}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{n,n-m}$ and $\ket{\phi^+_{\mu, a^{\lambda}_{\mu}}}\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes m+1}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{m+1}^{(d)}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{n,n-m-1}$ by
\@@amsalign
\end{Lem}\@proof SincethedualCGcoefficientsatisfies[seeEq.\penalty\ (10),p.289ofRef.\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{klimyk1995representations}{}{}]}]\begin{aligned} \tilde{C}^{\mu,\emptyset,\lambda,\emptyset}_{u^{\prime},j,u}=\sqrt{m_{\lambda}^{(d)}\over m_{\mu}^{(d)}}\left(C^{\lambda,\mu}_{u,j,u^{\prime}}\right)^{*},\end{aligned}wecanshowthat\begin{aligned} &[\mathds{1}_{d}^{\otimes m}\otimes(\mathrm{CG}^{-}_{n,n-m,d})^{\dagger}](\ket{\phi^+_{\lambda, a}}\\
&=\sqrt{1\over m_{\lambda}^{(d)}}\sum_{u}(\ket{\lambda,u}_{\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}^{(d)}}\otimes\ket{\lambda,a}_{\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}})\otimes(\mathrm{CG}^{-}_{n,n-m,d})^{\dagger}(\ket{\mu, \emptyset}_{\mathcal{T}_{n,n-m-1}}\otimes\ket{\lambda,\emptyset}_{\mathcal{T}_{n,n-m}}\otimes\ket{u}_{\mathcal{U}_{m}^{(d)}})\\
